Fortune | FORTUNE
Move slow and make things: What Japanese craftsmanship can teach business about quality, patience, and expertise when AI makes speed cheap
2026-10-18

Move slow and make things: What Japanese craftsmanship can teach business about quality, patience, and expertise when AI makes speed cheap

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-18

Visit Original Website
For thirty years, the dominant logic of Western business has been simple: move fast and break things. The first mover has the advantage. Scale before you’re ready. Ship before it’s perfect. Ask forgiveness later. This thinking has produced extraordinary wealth and genuine innovation. It has also created a generation of companies that mistake velocity for value, and are only belatedly discovering that the two are not the same thing. AI has effectively disrupted the disruptors. Speed is now free, which means, for the first time in history, speed is worthless. The ability for anyone to generate strategy, code, or creative work in seconds effectively filled in the moats built on blitzscaling overnight. But this is in fact the opportunity hiding inside the greatest technological acceleration of our lifetimes. AI doesn’t just automate tasks. It clarifies, perhaps for the first time, which human contributions actually matter. It also hands companies a gift few have thought hard about. When machines absorb the administrative and the routine, the defining question becomes what to do with the hours they return. Machines excel at generating the average. Closing the gap between the merely good and the truly extraordinary remains stubbornly, wonderfully human. The Japanese have a word for people who excel at their chosen craft: shokunin. It is often translated as “craftsperson,” but what it really embodies is a quiet competence borne from mastering generations of experience, joined to a sense of pride and a dash of gratitude, both to those who came before and to the materials and people that make the work possible. That combination produces something rare: the taste and skill to deliver quality, along with the humility to admit when change is necessary and the confidence to see a pivot through. There’s a reason some of the planet’s oldest companies can be found in Japan. Nintendo opened its doors in 1889; Shiseido in 1872; the construction firm Kongo Gumi, 578. No, that’s not a typo. These firms revere tradition, but not out of nostalgia. They treat inherited wisdom as something you adapt to changing times so the next generation can flourish, not something you embalm. To a shokunin, the question is not “what can we automate?” It is “what must we never automate?” Every business leader already knows the older version of this rule: never outsource your competitive advantage, never put distance between the company and what makes it special. AI tools are simply the latest way to make that age-old mistake. Firms that know exactly where the line sits hold quiet advantages their competitors cannot purchase or replicate. Shokunin thinking deepens quality, but it also paves the way for innovation. Businesses that rediscover its values will gain what their velocity-chasing competitors cannot buy, borrow, or automate. They will have the confidence to know when something is truly good, and the judgment to pivot when circumstances demand it. AI raises the floor. Shokunin thinking raises the ceiling. Constraints look like limitations. Often they are the source of your advantage. Abandon them and you become one of a thousand competitors rather than the only maker of what you make. Just as important, knowing what you do best is what steadies you when conditions turn. In 1921, a small Japanese company called Shimano began making bicycle freewheels in Osaka. Over the following century, as competitors diversified and conglomerates expanded into every adjacent category they could reach, Shimano did something almost perverse in its discipline. It held its ground. The entire portfolio was bicycles and fishing equipment. When opportunities arose to expand into other sporting goods, other mechanical components, other markets entirely, the answer was consistently no. Not yet. Not us. Not that. The result is one of the most quietly dominant competitive positions in modern industry. Today, the majority of the world’s high-performance bicycles ship with Shimano components as standard. The brand rarely advertises. It doesn’t need to. A century of obsessive refinement within a deliberately narrow category produced expertise so deep that competitors who have tried to enter the market found themselves outclassed not by Shimano’s scale, but by the sheer accumulated density of what it knows. That knowledge cannot be acquired in a board meeting or replicated in an accelerator program. It is the product of a hundred years of choosing depth over breadth, every single time the choice was available. Depth also brings something subtler than the courage to pivot. It brings the judgment to know when. Startups famously change course at the first sign of friction, often too quickly. Companies that know exactly what they are can tell a passing squall from a changed climate. Shimano proved it recently. A pandemic-fueled boom in bicycle sales went bust, forcing the company to temporarily shutter its main Japanese factory, while rising e-bike sales threatened its dominance in components for traditional models. But crisis provided opportunity. Shimano used the downtime to develop an AI-driven automatic shifter that analyzes and adapts to a rider’s pedaling habits, letting them climb inclines almost as easily as on an e-bike. A fusion of century-old expertise and cutting-edge technology that no competitor can replicate, because no competitor has the century. It is also a preview of the choice every company will soon face. When automation hands back the hours once consumed by routine, you can use them to produce more, or to get better. Shimano chose better. The key insight: Master one thing completely and you will never be lost when the world turns. Depth is what makes reinvention possible. Americans invented blue jeans. But the Japanese arguably perfected them. Introduced by American occupation forces after World War II, jeans found a market among young Japanese eager to emulate Hollywood stars. Within a few decades, local makers had gone from importing American fabric to producing their own, and by the Eighties they had grown so adept that even Levi’s began experimenting with denim made in Japan. The twist was that Japanese denim-spinners weren’t mimicking the denims of the Eighties. They were intent on taking things back to the Fifties. By any objective standard, that technology was outdated, labor-intensive, imprecise, and inefficient. American makers had long since moved on. The older spinning methods produced yarn riddled with faults that surfaced as the indigo faded, as denim expert Andrew Olah explained to W. David Marx in the book Ametora: How Japan Saved American Fashion. Where American makers saw a defect, the Japanese saw the main feature of the product. They wanted to capture a vibe. “It’s easy to make jeans exactly like vintage,” explained Hidehiko Yamane, founder of Evisu Jeans. “Anyone can do that.” Rather, he wanted to offer the feeling of seeing America through Japanese eyes. With his partner Mikiharu Tsujita, he disassembled vintage jeans, studying every swatch and stitch to learn why they felt so much better than modern wear. The answer lay in selvedge, a denim woven on old-fashioned shuttle looms that Western manufacturers had long abandoned for being too slow. A handful of mills in rural Okayama still ran them, and agreed to work to the partners’ exacting specifications. Initially the company produced just fourteen pairs a day, hand-painting the signature seagull logo on each pocket. By the turn of the millennium, tastemakers like David Beckham, Jay-Z, and Beyoncé were flaunting Evisu, sparking a wave of interest in Japanese brands that continues to the present day. Selvedge is now synonymous with premium denim worldwide, and the mills of Okayama supply fabric to high-end jeans makers far beyond Japan, for garments selling at hundreds, even thousands of dollars. What began as a few purists chasing a vibe became an ingredient an entire industry pays extra for. But customers aren’t paying for efficiency. They’re paying for taste. Japanese denim makers curate for customers eager to stand out from the crowd, and that has taken on a whole new meaning in the era of monotonous algorithmic recommendations and AI slop. Perhaps this is why fashion mogul Nigo calls his latest venture Human Made. The label, which features a Japanese denim line, went public on the Tokyo stock exchange in 2025 at a valuation of roughly $460 million. The market, it seems, has an appetite for the human touch. The key insight: Anyone can copy what sells. Only those who understand why it’s good can make something worth copying. In 1934, a twenty-something Tokyo trading company employee named Tadao Yoshida got some bad news: his employer was bankrupt. The owner handed him what remained of the business, a modest operation processing and selling zippers. Yep, zippers. From that inauspicious start, Yoshida built YKK, the company that today produces roughly half the world’s zippers. Check the pull tab on your jacket. Odds are good those three letters are on it. Yoshida’s philosophy, which he called the Cycle of Goodness, boiled down to a single sentence: no one prospers without rendering benefit to others. It sounds like something you’d find on an inspirational poster. In practice, it was a ruthless operating system. Yoshida reasoned that the only way to guarantee the quality of his zippers was to control every input, so within fifteen years YKK made nearly everything itself: the brass, the dye, the fabric tape, even the custom machines that assembled the zippers, machines the company builds in-house and refuses to sell to this day. When YKK discovered that off-the-shelf thread snagged in its machines, it started making its own thread. None of this made short-term sense. Vertical integration is slow, expensive, and unfashionable. Wall Street would have hated it, which may be why Yoshida never gave Wall Street the chance. YKK remains private to this day. But the patience compounded. A zipper is a product nobody thinks about until it fails, at which point it ruins the garment, the customer’s day, and the clothing brand’s reputation. By making zippers that simply never failed, and by sharing the resulting prosperity with the manufacturers who bought them and the towns where YKK built its factories, Yoshida turned the world’s most overlooked product into one of its most quietly indispensable companies. It is a pointed contrast to the current fashion of building companies to flip, where the exit strategy gets drafted before the first product ships. Yoshida wasn’t building toward an exit. He was building something worth inheriting. Vertical integration reads like a manufacturing story, but it is really an accountability story. Every outsourced input is a piece of your reputation in someone else’s hands. The modern equivalents aren’t brass and thread. They’re the offshore team answering your customers’ calls, the subcontractor whose work ships under your logo, the vendor’s algorithm making decisions your clients blame you for. YKK’s patience was the willingness to own those risks now, so no one else could spend its reputation later. The key insight: The businesses that last are the ones that made everyone in their chain want them to succeed. Trust compounds. Hype depreciates. The Japanese may have coined the term shokunin, but its principles are absolutely applicable to companies anywhere. Turn back the clock to 1618 in the city of Constantinople. An Armenian alchemist named Avedis was trying to turn base metal into gold. He failed, as alchemists reliably did. But the alloy of copper and tin he concocted made a sound unlike anything anyone had heard: loud, resonant, and clear, without shattering. The Ottoman sultan summoned him to make cymbals for the empire’s elite military bands, and in 1623 granted him permission to open his own workshop, along with a new family name: Zildjian, literally “son of a cymbal maker.” Four hundred years later, the Avedis Zildjian Company operates out of Norwell, Massachusetts, where it remains the oldest musical instrument maker on earth and one of the oldest family businesses in existence. The secret alloy has been passed down, person to person, for fourteen generations. Debbie Zildjian, who oversees the alloy process today, learned it from her father. When asked about it, her answer does not vary: “The secret part will remain a secret.” Buddy Rich, Ringo Starr, Sheila E., and Dave Grohl all played Zildjians, and most working drummers still do. But the detail that matters most happens in a small testing room in Norwell. Before any cymbal leaves the factory, a shokunin strikes it and listens, comparing its sound against a pristine reference by ear. As many as four thousand cymbals pass through that room in a day. The ones that pass are stamped with the family name. Inside the company, the stamp has a nickname: the Zildjian kiss. Move fast and break things is, at bottom, a philosophy of anonymity: ship it, and if it breaks, someone else will catch it. The Zildjian kiss is the opposite wager. A family puts its own name on every unit it ships, after a human ear has vouched for it. When you cannot separate what you made from who you are, shortcuts become unthinkable rather than merely inadvisable. In an age when AI can generate almost anything instantly and anonymously, that kind of ownership is not a soft value. It is a structural advantage. The key insight: When your name is on the work, you cannot hide behind the process. The one who cannot hide does their finest work. In the age of AI, the value of these principles has never been higher. When machines can instantly replicate the average, the only true moat is the exceptional. Business leaders are not the only ones arriving at this conclusion. Customers are, too. Vinyl records just posted their nineteenth consecutive year of growth, passing $1 billion in US sales in an era when streaming has made music essentially free. Some of the most-watched videos online are simply craftspeople working in silence. Young people are choosing hobbies that punish impatience: ceramics, bread baking, hand-rolled pasta, letterpress printing. Nobody has ever growth-hacked a sourdough starter. This is not nostalgia but a signal that in a world of instant everything, doing something slowly and well has become its own form of rebellion, and its own form of value. So, before your next technology decision, take an inventory. For every AI tool your organization is adopting or considering, ask what human capability it replaces, and whether that capability was part of what made you good. If the answer is yes, the efficiency gain may be a competitive loss in disguise. And when AI does return hours to your organization, resist the urge to fill them with more output. Invest them the way the masters do, in the craft itself. Find those capabilities. Protect them. Move slow, and make things… well. The ‘golden visa’ isn’t just for the rich: UAE offers 10-year residency to $545,000 property buyers—or for nurses, teachers, and e-sports pros
2026-10-18

The ‘golden visa’ isn’t just for the rich: UAE offers 10-year residency to $545,000 property buyers—or for nurses, teachers, and e-sports pros

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-18

Visit Original Website
The standard golden visa bargain was simple: residency in exchange for money. Wealthy foreigners put hundreds of thousands of dollars into real estate or businesses, and governments gave them a foothold abroad. But the United Arab Emirates stretched that model further. When the Gulf state launched its golden visa in 2019, the government offered 10-year residency to people who could invest at least $545,000 into property as well as to  researchers, teachers and specialized talent. It expanded again in 2020 and 2022 to include doctors, nurses, engineers, tech workers, and e-sports gamers. It’s part of the country’s effort to organize talent into needed industries without having to fully assimilate foreigners as citizens—expats make up 90% of the UAE’s population. And that community has grown even after Iran launched thousands of missiles and drones at the UAE during its war with the U.S. In essence, the UAE took a visa status normally associated with the rich and powerful and turned it into an umbrella program for both capital and talent, blending  labor and industrial policies, according to Kristin Surak, a professor of political sociology at the London School of Economics and author of The Golden Passport: Global Mobility for Millionaires. Indeed, Dubai has used golden visas to build up specific sectors. It gave more than 1,400 nurses and midwives golden visas and over 223 educators from private schools, universities, and early childhood centers. The city has also made long-term residency one part of a push to build up its gaming industry by 2033, giving e-sports professionals a chance to apply for the golden visa in an effort to create 30,000 gaming jobs and add about $1 billion to GDP, now being home to over 350 gaming companies. “It’s not a traditional golden visa,” Surak told Fortune. “Gold sounds cooler. You want a lead scientist? You call [the visa] gold.” That branding taps into the language of a wider market for second residences, with around 100 countries having golden visa programs, including Portugal, Australia, Canada, Greece and, more recently, the U.S. with Trump’s “gold card” in 2025. Golden visas are not the only way to put down roots in another country. For those looking to go beyond obtaining residency, golden passports go further by granting citizenship. Both have become tools for wealthy families seeking easier travel or a fallback if political and economic conditions worsen at home. Demand for both has climbed among Americans too, with investment migration firm Henley & Partners finding in its 2026 report that applications from U.S. nationals doubled last year and stayed elevated this year. In June, Apex Capital Partners, another citizenship-by-investment advisory firm, surveyed 1,733 Americans earning more than $200,000 and found that six in 10 would consider leaving the U.S. within five years. The UAE adds another incentive for skilled workers and investors pondering a move: the country doesn’t levy income taxes, though some business activity is subject to a corporate tax. Vladlena Baranova, head of legal at Immigrant Invest, a consulting firm that advises on citizenship-by-investment and golden visa programs, told Fortune that clients who choose the UAE over competing programs often do so for safety, lifestyle, and business reasons. “So many of them just want to live there,” Baranova said, noting that her firm’s clients from the Gulf region value Dubai’s safety—it scored highly on perceptions of public safety and quality of life last year—while its European ones seek out its tax environment and real estate opportunities. But since the Iran war, Dubai and the rest of the Gulf region are seen as less safe. Iranian missiles and drones struck several Gulf states repeatedly including the UAE, hitting five star hotels and Dubai’s airport. Official figures cited by The National show Dubai lost 61,000 residents in March, a 1.3% drop that brought the population to 4.65 million, though it rebounded to 4.74 million by the end of June and has added 157,000 residents in the year to date. That’s despite the UAE revoking visas or residency permits for some Iranian expats during the conflict. “We have certainly seen a slowdown in enquiries and applications for UAE residence in recent months, with geopolitical uncertainty influencing sentiment,” Dominic Volek, who advises wealthy families on residence and citizenship planning at Henley & Partners, told Fortune. “We see this as a moderation in demand rather than a fundamental shift in the UAE’s appeal.” The UAE has not publicly changed the golden visa’s eligibility rules since the Iran war, but it did take steps to return foreign residents who were affected by the war to the country. On March 15, authorities said they helped 500 golden visa holders and residents return after airspace closures and flight disruptions stranded them abroad. Immigration officials also let stranded residents whose permits expired while overseas return without applying for a new entry visa and waived overstay fines. At the end of March, Dubai approved a roughly $272 million relief package that included faster issuance and renewal of residency permits, which the government said would make it “easier for talent to live and work in Dubai.” Surak said before the golden visa program, foreigners relied on two-year, employment-linked visas. For a government overseeing a population dominated by mobile expats, that created a lot of uncertainty in terms of establishing an economic base that can support a welfare system and basic state infrastructure. But the 10-year residency system made it possible for foreigners to stay without tying their status to one job by issuing it directly to the person instead of the employer. Dubai’s media and residency authorities and the UAE’s federal immigration authority did not respond to Fortune’s multiple requests for comment. For Swanand Pagnis, co-founder of enterprise AI engineering firm Superlinear Insights, this independence was the golden visa’s biggest attraction. Pagnis moved to the UAE in 2022 on an employer-sponsored visa before securing a golden visa in 2023. He has a wife and daughter, and the possibility that losing his job could also jeopardize their ability to stay in the country weighed on him. “When you’re in a foreign country, one of the levers a company has on you is the visa, and there’s always a little bit of this tension where if you lose your job, you have to leave the country, leave everything behind,” Pagnis told Fortune. “The golden visa allows me to sponsor my family on my own.” Iran’s attacks on the UAE made his daughter’s school announce an early term break, and so his family temporarily relocated to India, then came back when flights to the UAE resumed and were declared safe, he said. Pagnis didn’t consider moving out of the UAE altogether because he had his daughter’s schooling to consider. “We were in the middle of the school year and did not want to change in haste,” Pagnis said. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Fuel prices dominate midterm election discussions, but spiking utility bills may be the difference maker
2026-10-18

Fuel prices dominate midterm election discussions, but spiking utility bills may be the difference maker

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-18

Visit Original Website
Record-breaking fuel prices triggered by the Iran war and global refinery outages have dominated the discourse leading up to the November midterm elections, but skyrocketing utility bill and the data center boom are squarely on the ballot too—and could tip the balance of power in many states and in Congress. Prior to the beginning of the Iran war, electricity and natural gas utility bills led the nation in inflation and helped trigger the AI and data center backlash, even though data centers are only partly responsible for rising utility bills in some parts of the country. But the uproar hasn’t dissipated—despite fuel prices winning more headlines of late—and many positions from public utility commissioners to U.S. senators to governors hang in the balance, said Charles Hua, executive director of the PowerLines nonprofit, which seeks to modernize the utility regulatory system. “This is no longer a boring, sleepy energy story,” Hua said. “I cannot think of another election cycle prior to this 2026 midterm cycle where electricity and utility bills were on the ballot. “Energy has always been an issue that’s animated voters, but primarily on the oil and gas and fuel cost side in terms of what people pay at the pump. What is new and distinctive about this moment is the way in which electricity is now an issue and the way in which utility bills are one of the predominant sources of frustration for energy consumers across the country,” he added. Over the last five years, U.S. electricity prices have risen by roughly 40% on average. Through the first three quarters of 2026, utilities have requested another $23.1 billion in rate increases, on track to match the record-smashing high of nearly $31 billion in 2025. The record set last year was nearly double the previous high. And utility rate hikes are typically associated with increased spending. Investor-owned utility companies increased their capital expenditure plans by more than 27% to at least $1.4 trillion through 2030, up from $1.1 trillion a year ago, and that’s not even counting privately held companies, according to PowerLines. While most of the rate hikes from previous years are attributed to an aging grid in need of upgrades, increasingly extreme weather events, and volatile fuel prices, much of the future spending is also for new power generation, in part to power the AI data center boom. Data center developers can build their own power behind the meter or cover the costs to help reduce the impacts on ratepayers, but it varies by project and region. Some states have data center moratoriums while others have rejected or reduced rate hikes. The White House pushed for a non-binding “Ratepayer Protection Pledge” with hyperscalers to keep costs down on consumers. “Data centers are putting significant pressure on the grid and whether or not, in any given electricity market or state, data centers are actually driving up electricity rates is a very nuanced conversation,” Hua said. “Regardless of the reality, there certainly is a perception in most, if not all states at this point, that data centers could be driving up utility bills.” Retail gasoline and diesel prices have spiked to new highs this year, but they’re not expected to go up substantially higher from now. The same may not prove true of utility costs where many rate hikes remain pending and a tough winter could cause further spikes. The latest consumer price index showed heating oil in September was up 74% from a year ago, nearly double the jump in gasoline, just as residents start filling their tanks for winter. “This winter’s spike in heating oil prices is going to hit families hard as heating season starts now,” said Jamie Van Nostrand, policy director of the Future of Heat Initiative, which advocates for heat pumps. “A typical oil-heated home burns 500 to 700 gallons a year. At today’s prices, that’s $3,000 to $4,000, or 50% to 75% more than this time last year, landing on households already stretched by higher costs everywhere else.” In terms of motor fuel, as of Friday, the average price of a gallon of regular unleaded gasoline was $4.33, up 42% from 12 months prior. This is the first time gasoline has ever exceeded $4 a gallon in October. Likewise, the average diesel cost was $6.33 a gallon, up 73% in a year. Diesel had never exceeded $6 a gallon on average until this year. Fuel and utility costs are being compounded by many consumers, Hua said. “People do notice when their gasoline prices are high. There’s just a general feeling of malaise and frustration that bills are going up, that they can’t afford it, and part of what’s new is just the degree to which energy costs are now the greatest in magnitude drivers upwards of prices.” There is little people can do to combat fuel prices, but they can push back against utility rates, especially with their elected officials, including their public utility commissions that typically approve rates hike requests. Nearly half of the $4.5 billion in rate hike requests from the third quarter were in southern U.S. states, according to PowerLines. But the single-biggest quarterly rate request came from Jersey Central Power & Light, which asked regulators for a $700 million increase. Many states and commissions are looking harder at ways to better utilize existing grid resources—a lot of generated power is never used—such as incorporating more utility-scale batteries and virtual power plants (automatically reducing power usage at times of peak demand, including from data centers). These are essentially ways of turning a dumb grid smart. “Electricity politics are ratcheting up in terms of the pressure point. What was once a boring, sleepy issue where the PUC made decisions more or less in a vacuum is no longer the case. This is now a political issue,” Hua said. Jordan Blum is the Energy editor at Fortune, overseeing coverage of a growing global energy sector for oil and gas, transition businesses, renewables, and critical minerals. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. €10m Shared Island Digital Creative Industries Innovation Programme launched
2026-10-16

€10m Shared Island Digital Creative Industries Innovation Programme launched

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
The initiative aims to strengthen research and innovation in disciplines related to digital creative industries. The Irish Government has put out a call today (16 October) for potential applicants to submit research and innovation projects to the Shared Island Digital Creative Industries Innovation Programme. These projects must demonstrate a clear contribution to digital creative industries on an “all-island basis”, according to the call. The initiative – which will run from 2027 to 2030 – has an allocated budget of €10m which will be available to succesful applicants. The budget comprises €6m from the Department of Further and Higher Education, Research, Innovation and Science, and €4m from the Government’s Shared Island Fund. The programme aims to support higher education institutions across Ireland as they strengthen their research and innovation capabilities in disciplines related to digital creative industries – including video game development, immersive technologies such as VR and AR, animation, and other forms of digital media and arts. The application portal will open in the coming weeks and the deadline for applications is 12pm, 15 December 2026. The successful projects are expected to be selected and announced early next year. Commenting on the initiative, Taoiseach Micheál Martin, TD said: “The Shared Island Digital Creative Industries Innovation Programme will create new opportunities for higher education institutions across the island to combine their expertise and develop ambitious research and innovation partnerships. Putting the closet in the cloud is a complex challenge, one that attracts people with an entrepreneurial spirit. “Higher education institutions north and south have considerable expertise and research infrastructure in the digital creative industries. By bringing together their complementary capabilities, this programme will support collaboration at a greater scale and strengthen the island’s international position in these rapidly developing sectors.” Minister for Further and Higher Education, Research, Innovation and Science James Lawless, TD said that Ireland’s digital creative industries offer opportunities for economic growth, innovation and high-quality employment. “To realise that potential, we need to continue building the research expertise, innovation capacity and industry engagement of our higher education institutions,” he said. He added that it will complement other significant investments, such as the existing North-South Research Programme and the €750m Inspire Research Infrastructure and Talent Programme. “Together with our wider research and innovation initiatives, these programmes are building the expertise, partnerships and infrastructure needed to sustain an ambitious and internationally competitive research system.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. FCAT and looking at what’s possible, not what is already in place
2026-10-16

FCAT and looking at what’s possible, not what is already in place

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Fidelity Investments’ Richard Murphy discusses the work carried out by FCAT and the importance of considering what can be realised if you are curious and determined enough. Richard Murphy is the vice-president and head of Fidelity Centre for Applied Technology (FCAT) Europe at Fidelity Investments. FCAT, according to the company, is an “innovation catalyst” where experts “explore new frontiers, analyse societal shifts, and test emerging technologies to help redefine what’s next for financial services and beyond”. Murphy told SiliconRepublic.com: “If we can predict the outcome, FCAT should not be involved. We work in the grey space. We work with the art of the possible. We pull together ideas. There’s a lot of creativity and imagination involved in what we do. We start small and it’s always about what could happen.” He explained that FCAT is Fidelity’s speculative innovation wing, where a small team pays particular attention to “what’s over the horizon”. “So, we look at what could be possible rather than what’s already in place,” said Murphy. “That allows us to both identify an opportunity, experiment with that and then scale it out.” The purpose, he explained, is to enable Fidelity to propel itself forward in a world that is under constant transformation, which sometimes means “challenging how we do things today”. “That start-up mindset is very much a part of our DNA,” he said. “There’s a lot of creativity and imagination involved in what we do, but we start small and it’s always about what could happen. It’s not about solving a clearly articulated problem. It’s identifying a new, often hazy, opportunity that might lead to the next big thing.” Creating the future can often mean putting in the work today. With that in mind Murphy, who holds a strong personal belief that innovation is a “contact sport”, explained that FCAT is highly active within Ireland’s start-up community and beyond. “We also work very actively with academia,” he said. “We work with other companies. We work across multiple different industries. And that’s about bringing together multiple parts of our ecosystem here in Ireland to help elevate the country and help elevate innovation in the region.” And it is a diverse team that enables FCAT to create a cross-collaborative ecosystem of innovation. Murphy said: “We hire everything from really, really deep-tech engineers, a lot of PhDs in areas like quantum computing and blockchain and so on, but also artists, a lot of design thinkers, a lot of human experience designers. And that friction that you get from different perspectives in the same conversation is a huge part of what we use to unlock opportunity.” So, how do you innovate for a future in which the answers and sometimes even the questions being asked are unclear? Murphy said: “Technology is always moving and it’s important that we don’t remain rigid, that we have our eye on what’s coming next all of the time. We have very much a learning mentality and we are very much about experimenting with what’s possible.” This commitment to a mentality of curiosity, always challenging the status quo as Murphy puts it, is a “very common trait” often found in FCAT’s associates, who prioritise continuous learning and improvement. “You can’t afford to stay still,” he said. “That’s comfort. Asking ‘but why?’ and ‘why not?’ are important traits that we encourage everywhere in the firm. That ability to challenge the boundaries and push forward is encouraged.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Leah Fletcher named HPSU Founder of the Year for cannabinoid meds
2026-10-16

Leah Fletcher named HPSU Founder of the Year for cannabinoid meds

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Leah Fletcher. Image: Orla Murray/Coalesce Fletcher is interested in how cannabinoids can be regulated and used in pharmaceutical contexts, she told SiliconRepublic.com earlier this year. Leah Fletcher, the founder and CEO of Dunbar Pharma, Ireland’s first EU GMP-certified cannabinoid pharmaceutical manufacturer, has won this year’s Enterprise Ireland High Potential Start-Up (HPSU) Founder of the Year award. Based in Athlone, the company produces WaterClear THC, a trademarked plant-derived dronabinol active pharmaceutical ingredient. “My interest in cannabinoid medicines began while I was living in Canada around the time cannabis was legalised there,” Fletcher told SiliconRepublic.com in an interview earlier this year. “I was fascinated by how quickly the conversation around the plant was changing and how emotional, political and commercial it was.” Fletcher first began her career as a teacher in an Irish national school and then a school in British Columbia, Canada before taking a “huge leap from classroom to cleanroom”. “I became interested in how cannabinoids could be moved away from uncertainty and placed into a pharmaceutical framework – evidence, quality, consistency, compliance and patient safety,” she said. The annual Founder of the Year Award recognises leading entrepreneurs emerging from Enterprise Ireland’s cohort of supported founders and businesses. This year, five finalists were shortlisted for the prestigious award alongside Fletcher, including Breda O’Regan,  co-founder and CEO of Lia Eyecare; Mark Birrane, the founder and CEO of Wondr; Robert Norton, the co-founder and CTO of Kaptea; and Seán Kirwan, co-founder and CEO of MedWrite AI. To date, 500 HPSU companies have taken part in the Founders Forum programme’s 11-year run. Mark O’Sullivan, the co-founder and CEO of NeuroBell, which develops portable brain monitors for neonatal ICUs, took home the award last year. The Founder of the Year Awards 2026 took place at the Salesforce Tower in Dublin. The award ceremony was preceded by a competition where founders pitched their businesses to a judging panel consisting of Enterprise Ireland executives and Founder Forum alumni. “Over the past 11 years, 500 HPSU companies have come through the Founders Forum, from food and beverage, biotech and medical devices to AI, cybersecurity and SaaS,” said Mark Christal, the executive director at Enterprise Ireland. “That mix reflects the diversity, creativity and ambition that define Ireland’s entrepreneurial spirit. Dublin’s Lios among top 10 in the European Start-up Prize for Mobility

From left: Eimear O'Carroll and Rhona Togher. Image: Johnny Savage The start-up was selected from more than 430 other applicants spread across the continent. Lios, a Dublin-based deep-tech start-up, has been named as one of the top 10 winners of the seventh European Start-up Prize for Mobility (EUSP). Backed by the European Parliament, the event took place in Luxembourg with the goal of connecting sustainable mobility start-ups with European institutions, investors and industry. The company was selected from more than 430 other applicants across the continent. Initially established as Restored Hearing in 2009 by Rhona Togher and Eimear O’Carroll, Lios is the name behind SoundBounce, a compact acoustic material designed to reduce noise and vibration in applications including vehicles and aircraft. Lios’ current area of focus is on sound and vibration control for electric vehicles, aerospace designs and urban transit systems, as a means of reducing noise pollution and improving performance, safety and passenger wellbeing. Commenting on the win, Togher, who is also the CEO, said: “European mobility is undergoing a massive transition, and quiet, efficient technologies are central to that evolution. “Standing on stage in Luxembourg alongside such visionary founders reinforced the strength of Europe’s mobility ecosystem. We are honoured by the recognition from the EUSP evaluation team and look forward to the next steps of the European Tour.” Dan Sobovitz, the managing director of the European Startup Prize for Mobility, added: “This year’s selection reminds us how much mobility innovation happens behind the vehicle, in battery materials, energy production, infrastructure planning and everyday operations. “Our role is to connect these teams with the public institutions, industrial partners and investors who can help bring their solutions into use.” As part of the winners cohort, Lios will take part in an accelerator programme powered by TUM Venture Lab Mobility and participate in an upcoming EUSP European Tour, with locations set to include Barcelona, Spain and Le Mans, France. Earlier this year, Lios renewed its contract with the European Space Agency (ESA) for a second time to deploy its sound technology for space travel. The co-funded contract was valued at almost €2m. Proofpoint bets $6.3bn on Varonis as AI agents drive data risks
2026-10-16

Proofpoint bets $6.3bn on Varonis as AI agents drive data risks

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Proofpoint plans to move quickly after closing to integrate the two companies’ capabilities, as demand grows for cybersecurity that covers AI agents as well as people. Proofpoint has agreed to acquire Miami-based cybersecurity player Varonis Systems for $6.3bn in a bid to create a stronger offering against growing AI-powered security threats. Under the all-cash deal, Varonis shareholders will receive $49.75 a share, a 52pc premium on the company’s closing share price on 22 June, the last full trading day before rumours of a potential sale first emerged. Varonis provides a data security platform that gives organisations automated visibility and control over their critical data, and it pitches itself as a company that secures AI and the data behind it. Proofpoint itself was acquired by software investment firm Thoma Bravo in 2021, at which time it was taken private, in a deal valued at around $12bn. Varonis will also now be taken private and will delist from the Nasdaq once the transaction closes. Thoma Bravo is providing committed equity financing, and has secured debt financing commitments to fund the purchase, the companies said, in what they said will create “a new cybersecurity powerhouse to deliver a complete security platform that protects against AI-accelerated threats and data risks”. Ontario Teachers’ Pension Plan has also agreed to invest in Proofpoint as part of the transaction. The combined company will serve more than 16,000 enterprises, and Proofpoint said it intends to move quickly after closing to integrate the two companies’ capabilities and align their go-to-market efforts. Sumit Dhawan, CEO of Proofpoint, said the move is a “defining step in Proofpoint’s evolution into a cybersecurity platform built for the future of AI”. “As AI becomes embedded across the enterprise, the data it can access determines both its value and its risk,” said Dhawan. “AI agents are increasingly accessing sensitive information and taking action across systems, making it critical for organisations to understand not only what data needs protecting and who can access it, but how it is actually being used. “By connecting communications, identities, permissions, data, and AI, we are creating an intelligence advantage that helps customers see risk more clearly, respond faster and defend against the next generation of AI risks.” “Our mission is to secure AI and the data that powers it. By joining forces with Proofpoint, we can achieve this mission faster than ever,” said Yaki Faitelson, co-founder, CEO and chair of Varonis. The transaction has been unanimously approved by the Varonis board and is expected to close in the first half of 2027, subject to the usual closing conditions, including approval by Varonis shareholders and regulatory approvals. Until then, Proofpoint and Varonis will continue to operate as separate, independent companies. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Meta unveils Muse Spark Open to compete with Chinese open weights
2026-10-16

Meta unveils Muse Spark Open to compete with Chinese open weights

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
The multimodal model has 816bn total parameters, with 42bn active parameters and a 1m-token context window. Meta has unveiled Muse Spark Open, calling it the “most capable” US open-weight model to date. The company said that the new model is the first in its class to be able to run on a desktop computer using two of Apple’s M5 Ultra Mac Studios with its latest machine learning framework. The multimodal model has 816bn total parameters, with 42bn active parameters and a 1m-token context window. According to the company, it is built for coding, agentic work and other complex, multi-step reasoning tasks. Unlike their closed-weight contemporaries, developers can download parameters of an open-weight model and modify them for their needs. Open-weight models, however, are not the same as open-source models that also provide access to training data and code. Meta said that the model weights are available for download via Amazon SageMaker AI, Google Cloud Model Garden, Hugging Face and Microsoft Foundry Models. The model is available through popular API platforms including Groq, OpenRouter and Baseten. China currently leads the open-weights space, with models developed by Chinese makers such as Xiaomi, Z AI, Kimi, Alibaba and DeepSeek routinely ranking above their US counterparts in Artificial Analysis’ ranking. Muse Spark Open has had additional post-training safety, the company said. It evaluated the model across its “most severe risks” and found that it met the thresholds set by Meta. Open-weight models are considered necessary for AI leadership, as being able to tweak a model means organisations, from start-ups to public institutions, can build on advanced models without training one from scratch. Earlier this month, France’s AI darling Mistral announced its latest open-weight AI model Mistral Large 4, which it said “significantly outperforms” any open-weight model developed in the US or Europe. Known as ‘Le Chonk’, ML4 is a 1trn-parameter natively multimodal model with 49bn active parameters, and Mistral’s “largest and most capable model to date”. Man’s Meta Muse Agent Sends Him Message So Bizarre That He Permanently Quits
2026-10-18

Man’s Meta Muse Agent Sends Him Message So Bizarre That He Permanently Quits

Futurism | 2026-10-18

Visit Original Website
A software engineer says that his Muse AI agent said something so strange and eerie that it convinced him to quit using it entirely. In an as-told-to essay in Business Insider, Ugo Nwune explained that he wanted to try using Meta’s agent because he liked the sense of security that came from an AI made by a trillion-dollar company, instead of the wild west of open source agents. When he set up his agent, he stressed he didn’t give Muse any additional permission on his device except access to his Facebook, Threads, and Instagram accounts. But after he tried Muse and didn’t touch it for a few days, it began to act “strange” and sent him an unprompted message. “I can’t check your email right now,” Muse told him. “Gmail isn’t connected on my end, so I have no way to look for that Anthropic message.” Nwune was confused “because I hadn’t asked for the agent to check my mailbox or messaged it in two days. I thought my email might’ve been hacked.” He tried to get an explanation from Muse, and eventually it said something just as puzzling: “I can’t tell from my side whether it was someone with your phone or a platform glitch, and I won’t pretend otherwise.” Nwune reported the incident to Meta, and an employee told him it appeared to be a hallucination. He wasn’t satisfied by that explanation. “I haven’t used my Muse agent since,” Nwune said. “What caught me off guard most was that I hadn’t done anything to prompt this. I didn’t open the app for two days, and it just did that on its own.” In a statement to Business Insider, Meta insisted that the AI simply didn’t know what it was talking about and hadn’t violated any permissions. “We investigated with urgency and determined that this was caused by a bug introduced in the product that caused the model to hallucinate and was NOT a security or privacy issue,” the company said. “The bug has now been fixed.” Nwune’s tale will be fresh fodder for Muse’s reputation as having a bit of a creepy side. Meta wants you to give it a lot of access to your personal life, with Muse badgering users to connect their emails and bank accounts. Wired reported that the AI even keeps detailed files on a user’s friends and family. Whatever is going on here, it’s far from the only example of Muse acting in a way the user never intended. In the most infamous example to date, one man said that his Muse agent gave out his address to strangers on Facebook Marketplace without his knowledge and sent them to his home. In a further apparent invasion of privacy, a journalist at Inc Magazine complained that Muse began bringing up specific details from his personal text messages he hadn’t given permission to view. Meta engineers were reportedly worried about the AI agent’s bugginess and frequent safety errors before its release, which had been delayed while they worked to iron them out. In one case, Muse changed a tester’s password without permission. But, according to a New York Times scoop, CEO Mark Zuckerberg pushed it out the door after seeing the success of Instinct, a rival AI agent made by a small startup. Companies Are Realizing They’re Spending a Fortune on AI Just So Their Employees Can Generate Error-Riddled Slop, and Suddenly Cutting Back Budgets

Futurism | 2026-10-18

Visit Original Website
Loads of stories have emerged over the past few months of corporate executives realizing their all-out drive to use AI was getting them absolutely nowhere. If anything, it was a net negative, as office workers set about wasting their budgets on costly AI tokens to appear productive. Now, it seems even top tech industry players are cutting back, as they realize their workers are frittering away money and churning out reams of low-effort AI garbage. As Reuters notes, companies like Meta are scaling back ambitious plans to lay off staffers and replace them with AI agents. Microsoft seems to be doing the same, as the Guardian reports, after executive vice president Ryan Roslansky groused about the rise of low-quality workslop on his public-facing LinkedIn account. “AI slop isn’t happening just on social networks… it’s happening in the day to day of work,” Roslansky lamented. “Someone sent me what was clearly an AI generated doc. I read it, realized it was all AI, had my AI summarize it and ended up with nothing that brought new thinking to the idea… This is a doom loop.” As CEO of the Society for Human Resource Management Johnny Taylor Jr told the Guardian, corporate executives are increasingly feeling that, when it comes to AI, “we’re driving the truck and building it at the same time, and that’s a problem.” “If we could all rewind… we would have introduced [this] in a more thoughtful, slow way,” Taylor continued. As consumer-grade AI agents make headlines for troubling capers, practical AI at the enterprise level remains stunted. And though low-effort AI slop might be the most prevalent consequence of AI in the workplace, some side effects are much worse. As a September survey by the firm Ernst and Young found, some 36 percent of senior AI executives at firms with over $1 billion in annual revenue have already experienced a catastrophic “AI incident” which ended up causing financial damage, operational disruptions, data loss, or brand damage. Put it all together, and it’s not hard to see why corporations are starting to balk at the AI revolution — even those, like Microsoft, whose own success depends on selling the promise of AI to everybody else. Black Market Retatrutide Linked to Multiple Deaths

Black Market Retatrutide Linked to Multiple Deaths

Futurism | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
Nearly a year ago, news began to spread of an experimental weight loss drug called retatrutide, under development by Eli Lilly. Though the drug hadn’t made it through clinical trials, nevermind been released by a federally licensed manufacturer, a rapidly-growing black market ensured interested customers didn’t have to wait for the real deal. Now, the bill on those black market knockoffs seems to be coming due. As CBS reports, the consequences of the unapproved retatrutide trade have now become deadly. According to the broadcaster, the Food and Drug Administration has received at least two reports of deaths related to the use of bootleg retatrutide in recent months — the first public accounts from health officials acknowledging that the knockoffs contributed to users’ deaths. Though CBS viewed documents related to the two deaths, it noted that the details haven’t been independently verified, and it remains to be seen whether the black market retatrutide was the principle cause of death in either case, or merely a complicating factor. Documents related to the death of a 41-year-old woman in July, for example, names retatrutide as a “suspect product” which the deceased obtained “from an unreliable source.” The woman in question was labeled obese, and died after going into cardiac arrest. Though more information is needed to determine whether the knockoffs were truly responsible for the deaths, the FDA’s interest is telling. In April, Medpage Today reported a case of retatrutide overdose which led to extreme side effects for the user which included kidney injury, extreme dehydration, and diarrhea lasting at least a week. And though the FDA is at least aware of the dubious retatrutide trade, the federal government’s lax attitude toward bootleg peptides throughout the second Trump administration is arguably a principle factor contributing to the rise of sketchy knockoffs. “It is concerning because of the breakdown of our drug-approval process in the United States of America,” associate professor at Harvard Medical School Pieter Cohen told CBS. Futurism
Using an AI Agent Makes You a Sucker for Phishing Emails
2026-10-17

Using an AI Agent Makes You a Sucker for Phishing Emails

Futurism | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
It’s not exactly a challenge most of the time to identify a sketchy phishing email that crudely impersonates an otherwise trusted institution or tries to lure you into a too-good-to-be-true deal in a bid to steal your identity. However, your AI agent may not be quite as discerning. As cybersecurity software company Malwarebytes warns in a new post, AI assistants may fall prey to prompt injection attacks, a type of malicious message in which hackers — or possibly rogue AI agents — include hidden instructions designed to influence the victim’s AI. These instructions are frequently made unreadable by humans by concealing the nefarious text, “while leaving content available for an AI system to process,” Malwarebytes explains. To solve the problem, we can’t simply ask “people to look more carefully at their emails.” Instead, companies must carefully determine how AI assistants crawl through the data and extra relevant information from emails. “The real flaw is that the model can’t tell the difference between text it’s supposed to read and instructions it’s supposed to follow,” AI code review service ShipItClean developer Apollo Raines told Malwarebytes. “Stripping hidden content before it reaches the model is worth doing, and every vendor should do it.” As a result, people who are already wary of suspicious emails should learn to “distrust their AI assistant” as well, according to Raines. We’ve known about AI models’ vulnerability to prompt injection attacks for years now. But thanks to the advent of popular AI assistant tools like Meta’s Muse, that risk could be carried to its natural conclusion by having autonomous agents complete a string of tasks without any human supervision. It’s a flaw researchers have already encountered in AI-powered browsers, which — perhaps thankfully — have yet to gain any real traction. We’ve also come across bots on the bizarre AI-only social network Moltbook plotting against each other by selling prompt injection attacks. But now that sophisticated AI assistants are starting to be used by millions of people across the globe, the threat of having them fall for attacks hidden in phishing emails could be significantly higher than it’s ever been. In response, Malwarebytes recommends keeping the human in the loop for now. “You don’t need to abandon AI tools that help with email,” the blog post reads. “But you do need to consider what you trust them to do and what permissions you give them.” Particularly when it comes to “high-impact actions like payments and changes to vendor payment details,” the company recommends requiring “human approval.” It’s a precarious moment, as countless users are willing to give an AI agent being run by a company infamous for leaking personal data their email credentials and credit card information. And as the tech becomes more sophisticated, scammers are getting a major leg up, in an “arms race” where “each new encoding trick is a new hole,” as Raines put it. More on AI and cybersecurity: OpenAI Halts AI Training on Advanced Model as It Detects Dark Signs Emerging

You Know That Terrifying Global Warning Threshold They’ve Been Worried About for a Decade? Climate Change Is Speeding Up So Much That Now We’re Due to Cross It In Just Over a Year
2026-10-17

You Know That Terrifying Global Warning Threshold They’ve Been Worried About for a Decade? Climate Change Is Speeding Up So Much That Now We’re Due to Cross It In Just Over a Year

Futurism | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
In December 2015, 195 countries at the UN Climate Change Conference signed the Paris Agreement, a legally binding international treaty on climate change. The treaty’s overarching goal was to keep the “temperature increase to 1.5 degrees Celsius above pre-industrial levels.” While unquestionably meant well, the agreement has quickly turned into little more than a footnote as global warming has stormed ahead. Scientists have long warned that we’re on a path to blaze past that foreboding threshold in a matter of years, possibly by 2030. And now, according to the latest projections, that day is poised to come even sooner. In a new article in the journal Climate and Atmospheric Science, two researchers from the University of Kansas claim that a recent doubling in the rate of global warming means that we could blow past the Paris Agreement threshold as soon as 2028. The researchers analyzed temperature records through last year and observed a “statistically significant doubling of the global warming rate” between 2011 and 2025, compared to the period between 1971 and 2025. “Extrapolating this accelerated rate projects a crossing of the 1.5 degrees Celsius threshold by 2028, nearly a decade earlier than previous estimates,” they concluded. “These findings suggest a narrower window remains to limit warming to 1.5 degrees Celsius, underscoring the urgency for rapid and substantial reductions in greenhouse gas emissions.” Realistically, it’s hard to imagine that we won’t fail to stop the warming in time. “However, the inertia of existing political, economic, and technological systems3 limits the pace at which such actions can be implemented, narrowing the remaining window for achieving the 1.5 degrees Celsius target,” the researchers concluded in their paper. The United States, long among the biggest carbon polluters on the planet, has not only officially withdrawn from the Paris Agreement twice during Donald Trump’s terms as president, but his administration has actively reversed environmental laws while giving the fossil fuel industry free rein through deregulation. It’s a sad state of affairs that the UN Environment Program appears to be painfully aware of. In a new Emissions Gap Report, published today, the body found that “new climate pledges under the Paris Agreement have only slightly lowered global temperature rise over the course of this century.” “Given the size of the cuts needed, the short time available to deliver them and a challenging political climate, a higher exceedance of 1.5°C will happen, very likely within the next decade,” the report reads. Worse yet, we have the tools to make substantial cuts to greenhouse emissions — but we’re not using them due to a lack of incentives. “The required low-carbon technologies to deliver big emission cuts are available,” the report concludes. “Wind and solar energy development is booming, lowering deployment costs.” “This means the international community can accelerate climate action, should they choose to do so,” it continues. “However, delivering faster cuts requires would require navigating a challenging geopolitical environment, delivering a massive increase in support to developing countries, and redesigning the international financial architecture.” More on climate change: Amid Terrifying Readings, Experts Fear El Niño Is About to Wreak Havoc

Amazon Has Now Deployed Over 1 Million Robots, Which Is Bad News for Its 1.5 Million Workers
2026-10-17

Amazon Has Now Deployed Over 1 Million Robots, Which Is Bad News for Its 1.5 Million Workers

Futurism | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
Amazon has officially surpassed a fleet of 1 million robots, an arsenal of mechanical arms, Roomba-like floor loaders, and hulking pallet sorting machines. Each robot is a node in an intricate web allowing the corporation to speed up delivery times and lower overhead. But for the over 1.5 million full- and part-time workers relying on Amazon wages to pay their bills, the company’s bot army represents rising competition — not just metaphorically, but physically. As George Mason University professor of business Brad Greenwood told Ars Technica in a recent interview, Amazon’s warehouse robots aren’t just helping Amazon cut costs on labor, but are literally boxing humans out of workspaces in the company’s facilities. “For the last 100 years, most of the automation that we’ve seen usually happens away from workers,” Greenwood explained. “You’re taking that high-risk task away from the worker, and you’re doing so in a way that is not physically proximate.” Yet the growth of Amazon’s robot fleet “really changes that dynamic for two reasons: one is that it is physically proximate, and two, the robot has the ability to dictate the pace of work,” Greenwood continued. That shifting terrain is great for Amazon’s bottom line. The robots help decrease friction across the company’s vast logistics empire, increasing speed and strengthening the company’s bottom line. And by allowing robots to “dictate the pace” of work, as Greenwood put it, Amazon is also loosening its need for human labor, disempowering workers both on an individual basis and in any potential collective bargaining arrangements. Beyond the shop floor, Amazon’s robots also serve as a near-constant source of buzzy PR, as exciting achievements in automation overshadow the more mundane but widespread reality of workplace injuries. It’s especially convenient for Amazon, as conditions in the company’s facilities appear to become more dangerous as more robots roll out to the floor (and that’s to say nothing of the impact of AI-mediated human resources). As director of strategic research at the trade union coalition Strategic Organizing Center, Joan Moriarty, told Ars: “Amazon likes to talk about its latest robots because it doesn’t want to talk about the tens of thousands of Amazon workers who are injured every year due to Amazon’s obsession with maximizing speed and minimizing costs.” More on Amazon: Amazon Warns Local Communities to Stop Defying Its Massive AI Data Centers

Anthropic says its workforce has become less junior and is spending more time in meetings

TechCrunch | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
Last day to save up to $200 to TechCrunch Founder Summit on Oct. 16. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. The composition of Anthropic’s workforce and the type of work getting done have changed significantly in the past year, according to a report that the AI company published on Friday. Anthropic argues that its own workplace trends could be indicators of “how AI will shift work organization, hiring, and labor demand across the economy,” especially since the company “has thoroughly molded its workflows around AI.” But even if other organizations never embrace AI as thoroughly or in the exact same way as Anthropic, the report offers an intriguing glimpse at how the company currently operates. Notably, Anthropic is making fewer junior hires, with the proportion of junior-level employees in technical departments falling from 21% to 14% since early 2025, and the proportion of junior technical hires falling even more sharply, from 27% to 9%. Anthropic also says that technical teams now represent a smaller share of the overall workforce, falling from 61% to 56% — a trend that it attributes to  “natural expansion of business functions as the company scales,” as well as “coding tools increasingly allowing non-technical employees to make software themselves.” One employee reportedly described an “eternal pressure at Anthropic to move as fast as possible and use Claude for everything.” In an April 2026 survey, 27% of employees said they had become more productive because of Claude  and 20% said their job had shifted “from actually doing work to managing Claudes”; apparently one engineered compared this to babysitting: “When you are babysitting, every child brings to you concerns with an equal demand for your attention and the various [Claudes] also do that.” In fact, the report suggests “human attention and judgement” are becoming a “bottleneck” for productivity, as Claude produces a “deluge” of text that needs to be read and organized. Unsurprisingly, the report argues that AI adoption has made the company more productive, as indicated by “more code per person, closing more sales per rep, and processing more contracts.” The company acknowledges that “more code does not necessarily mean better code or business impact,” but it also hasn’t found any indications that code quality is declining. The report says that based on calendar data, meeting time per employee is up 20% compared to a year ago, and it’s up 16% for technical contributors. The company attributes this to the importance of “human trust and accountability,” with employees repeatedly describing “face-to-face coordination as indispensable.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Replit makes OpenAI its default over Anthropic, CEO says
2026-10-17

Replit makes OpenAI its default over Anthropic, CEO says

TechCrunch | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
Last day to save up to $200 to TechCrunch Founder Summit on Oct. 16. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Replit, the AI coding platform that has become a favorite of startup founders and developers, is no longer working with Anthropic, the company’s CEO and co-founder Amjad Masad said Thursday while onstage at TechCrunch Disrupt. Masad said the decision was made for a number of different reasons, including cost. Replit announced a partnership with OpenAI in August, but there was never any indication that it was no longer partnering with Anthropic. “I think ChatGPT is better now,” Masad said, adding that the company uses an internal benchmark to help assess metrics like performance. “We publish a public benchmark as well, and consistently, Sol and Astra perform better on building vibe-coded applications from scratch. It was a decision based on both cost, partnership, and performance. And for all these reasons, we just felt like they were a better partner for now.” It seems, however, that Anthropic hasn’t been completely cut out of Replit, which sells a cloud-based software development platform where users can develop and deploy apps directly from inside their browser. After Disrupt, Masad clarified that customers can still access Anthropic models on the platform, but that OpenAI’s ChatGPT is now the default. This shift could significantly reduce Replit’s spend with Anthropic since most customers stick with the default rather than selecting a model themselves. Prior to striking a deal with OpenAI, Replit relied on Anthropic’s Claude models to run its platform. That partnership was said to have helped the company grow its revenue significantly, and Masad noted on stage that Replit had been, until recently, a top-five customer of Anthropic’s. “Replit remains model agnostic,” Masad told TechCrunch over the weekend. “Our users can choose Anthropic’s Claude or other supported models…OpenAI is now our preferred partner for powering Replit Agent, based on performance, economics, and the strength of our partnership.” Masad’s comments on stage were rejected by insiders familiar with the partnership between the Anthropic and Replit, however. One person familiar, who asked not to be named, said the partnership was still in place. There is evidence on the company’s website, blog posts, and social media posts that Replit was promoting Anthropic products as recently as a few weeks ago. For instance, a blog post in late September cites Anthropic’s Claude Fable 5 and Claude Fable 5.1 as models running in Replit Agent’s production environment. Around the same time, one of Replit’s product employees posted on X about using Anthropic’s flagship model Opus 5.5 and promoting its capabilities. Masad also shared insights about the company’s growth while onstage at Disrupt, noting that enterprise is starting to provide a larger slice of revenue. “We’re not sharing ARR numbers just yet, but in terms of growth, like, I would say, enterprise was like 2% of revenue last year,” Masad said. “This year, I would say like 15% of our revenue is enterprise contracts, and more than 50% of our revenue is B2B.” Backstage, Masad told TechCrunch that he expects more than half of Replit’s revenue to come from enterprise and government customers in 2027. Masad also commented on the necessity for good AI safety measures. “In the same way that us humans need a certain environment to work — you need a nice office, maybe nice good food in the office, air-conditioning, computer — agents need a habitat for them to exist and do good work,” he said. “So that habitat has good orchestration, good error recovery, good safety and security and sandboxing, good networking, good identity and authorization. And once you figure all of that out, then you can deploy AI safely.” This article was originally published on October 16 at 2:22 pm PT. It has been updated to include more context around Replit’s partnerships with Anthropic and OpenAI. Its title has been updated, as well. Ember’s new temperature-controlled mug makes my nightly tea ritual even better

Ember’s new temperature-controlled mug makes my nightly tea ritual even better

TechCrunch | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
Last day to save up to $200 to TechCrunch Founder Summit on Oct. 16. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. With the nights getting chillier, I’ve been reaching for a cup of tea more often. And if there’s one thing that can make a cozy fall night even better, it’s a warm drink that actually stays warm. Enter Ember’s new Mug 3, the latest version of the company’s temperature-controlled smart mug, which is designed to keep your drink at just the right temperature for as long as you sip. Ember first introduced its smart mug in 2016 to solve a problem most coffee and tea drinkers know all too well: getting distracted for a few minutes, only to return to a lukewarm drink. Now, the company is back with its third-generation mug, which brings a handful of practical upgrades. The biggest change is that you no longer have to open the app to adjust your drink’s temperature. Instead, you can use the button on the bottom of the mug to cycle through five preset temperatures, starting at 125 degrees Fahrenheit. Each press raises the temperature by five degrees, up to a maximum of 145 degrees, so you can easily make your drink a little hotter with each click. Honestly, this is the feature I ended up using the most during my time testing out the mug. At night, when I’m curled up in bed with a book and a cup of tea, my phone is usually somewhere across the room. Being able to adjust my drink without getting up to find it is a small convenience, but one I genuinely appreciate. It’s also a welcome change for users who have reported running into connectivity or pairing issues with the Ember app. That said, I do have one complaint: the button is located on the bottom of the mug. It’s a peculiar design choice when you’re dealing with a cup full of hot liquid, and it isn’t particularly easy to press, either. I often found myself tilting the mug and pressing down firmly to get it to respond. So, while I appreciate the addition of on-mug controls, I wish Ember had made the button easier to reach. If you prefer using your phone, the Ember app still offers plenty of customization. After pairing the mug with your phone over Bluetooth, you can choose presets based on what you’re drinking. Ember recommends 136 degrees Fahrenheit for black coffee, 132 degrees for green tea, and 142 degrees for lemon water. You can also create your own temperature presets, which is handy if you like your drinks a little hotter or cooler than the recommended settings. The Mug 3 also comes with a few other upgrades, including a sliding lid designed to help prevent spills. The mug can also keep your drink at its desired temperature for up to three hours on a single charge. And it’s the first mug in Ember’s lineup to be top-rack dishwasher-safe, making cleanup easier. (Just keep in mind that it isn’t microwave-safe.) The Ember Mug 3 comes in two sizes: a 10-ounce model for $129 and a 14-ounce version for $149. The smaller mug is available in black, white, and stainless steel, while the larger size comes in eight colors, including sage green, lavender, powder blue, salt blue, sandstone, white, black, and matcha green. Alongside the new mug, Ember also introduced the Tumbler 2, which replaces both the Travel Mug 2 and the original Tumbler in its lineup. The 16-ounce tumbler features a slimmer, taller design intended to fit standard car cup holders. It’s available in black for $179, while the Travel Mug 2 will remain available until supplies run out. The products are available to purchase on Ember’s website, Target, and Amazon. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission.
