It Was Elon Musk All Along
2026-10-17

It Was Elon Musk All Along

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
The Surge is your weekly political sanity check: What matters, what might, and what’s just political theater. Sign up here to get it in your inbox. Welcome to this week’s edition of the Surge, a politics newsletter that, since its redesign, is looking sharper than Jon Husted’s ass. We have only two-and-a-half more weeks until these damnable midterms are over, after which all politics will cease and this will become a newsletter about spelunking. Hooray! This week: Donald Trump is so mad about fuel prices screwing up the midterms that he might just give Europe to Russia. A White House press secretary has come and gone since we last wrote, and now she can return to being a lawyer (maybe?). Jon Ossoff said WHAT? There are competitive Senate races WHERE? But first! It was super PAC donor disclosure week, and you’ll never guess who’s spending the most money this election cycle. (Hint: It’s the guy with the most money.) Recall in the spring, during the Texas primary season, the big conundrum that Senate Republicans faced over money. Their concern was that if baggage-laden Attorney General Ken Paxton defeated Sen. John Cornyn for the nomination, the party would have to spend hundreds of millions of dollars to (attempt to) rescue him in the fall, money that it could be spending in traditional battlegrounds. Then, after Paxton rode Trump’s last-minute endorsement to win the primary, there was a lengthy standoff between irritated Senate Republicans and Trump’s super PAC over who should foot the bill to rescue Paxton. The conundrum was resolved around Labor Day. Trump’s MAGA Inc. has put $30 million into the state. But it’s Texas PAC, a spinoff of Senate Republicans’ Senate Leadership Fund, that’s done the bulk of the work, having poured $148 million into the state as of this writing. It both did and didn’t come as a surprise that Senate Republicans so easily let themselves be pushed around by Trump’s operation into cleaning up Trump’s mess. But with third-quarter fundraising filings now coming in, we can see the deal that was struck: Senate Republicans’ PAC spent the rescue money, but they were just a vessel. The guy really picking up the tab was Elon Musk. Senate Leadership Fund announced this week that it raised $273 million in the third quarter, with a full $158 million of that—58 percent—coming from Musk. In total, Musk has now given a jokelike $366 million this cycle, making him comfortably the biggest donor of either party. With that $158 million gift to the Senate Leadership Fund, he made months of Republican worries about how to (attempt to!) rescue Paxton look foolish. It was resolved with two checks, two strokes of the pen. No hard decisions over resources would have to be made after all. For all the attention Musk got during the 2024 campaign and the early part of Trump’s second term, it’s gone relatively unnoticed the staggering degree to which he’s shaping these midterms with his checkbook. That’s not by accident. He’s learned that people want his money but not him. From the 2024 election through the first few months of 2025, Musk was everywhere: On the campaign trail prancing about onstage with Trump, living at Mar-a-Lago, attending inauguration and inauguration rallies, shredding the federal government apart, fishing around the Treasury Department’s payment system, wielding chain saws at conservative conferences, and trying to win Wisconsin Supreme Court elections. Musk eventually crashed out and receded back to his companies (which were suffering in the stock market during his extracurricular activities) and said he would spend “a lot less” on politics going forward. “I think I’ve done enough,” he said in May 2025. (Over that summer, he and Trump would have a spectacular falling out over social media. They’ve since repaired things.) Musk, it turns out, has not spent “a lot less” on politics—if we’re talking dollars. He has now spent more on the midterms than he spent to help elect Donald Trump in 2024. He’s just done it more quietly, without attaching the force of his weird personality as a condition. He is not skipping around onstage behind Ken Paxton in Texas. He’s just funding his candidacy. In full. He has taken the most expensive race of the cycle off of Trump, the Senate GOP’s, and the candidate’s plate. We know it’s ho-hum to observe that there’s too much money in campaigns. But this is really something! Musk has reshaped the midterms with an amount of money that comfortably cruises past any other donor’s contributions this cycle but is effectively less than couch change to him—an amount of money he’s made in the time it’s taken us to write this (run-on, sure) sentence. Is that how our elections should work? Guess it depends on who you ask. For a while now, the Trump administration has been blaming the spike in diesel prices on Ukrainian attacks on Russian refineries. He’s not wrong that it’s a meaningful factor. But it’s an exacerbating factor atop the underlying cause of the Iran war. Trump can’t blame spiking prices on the Iran war, of course, since he was the one who done did it. Laying blame at the feet of the Ukrainians, whom MAGA has been unusually hostile to for years, serves the White House’s political interest heading into the midterms. And man, does that political interest suck for geopolitics. To spite Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky, Trump reached a diesel deal with Russia—just weeks after signing into law a bill allowing the president to tariff the top importers of Russian energy in the hopes of cutting off funding for the Russian war machine. (Trump has yet to announce any self-tariffs.) That indeed did tick off Zelensky, who then ticked off Trump, who said Ukraine needs a new president. Things have only gotten worse from there. The Ukrainians this week reported a disruption in their access to Starshield, the military-grade satellite network that Ukraine uses for battlefield communications and drone operation—and Reuters reported that the order to do this came from the White House. Zelensky appears to be finished pretending to be nice to Trump and has resumed attacks on Russian energy infrastructure against White House wishes. Say what you will about the guy, but Lindsey Graham’s back-channeling between the two is conspicuously absent right now. The worse the midterms go for Republicans, the wider the universe of blame Trump will cast. The only thing that’s certain—like, certain—is that Trump will not question whether he, by making extremely optional policy choices that raised the cost of living, contributed to the losses. No, the blame will be cast fully outward, from the inner rings of staff to political consultants, to candidates, to RINOs, to perceived Democratic cheaters. And it will be cast globally, to figures doing what is necessary to save their country from invasion. As a B-plot for the October of an election year, we might as well have a White House press secretary get hired and fired in the same week. So it went with Katie Zacharia, a member of the broader MAGA extended universe whose two or three public appearances in her new role were bad enough for Trump to ax her on Friday. This was all on top of questions about whether she was actually an attorney, a simple question that she had a difficult time answering. Who’s up next? Who cares? A lot less attention should be paid to who holds this position and whether they’re “good” at it. Being “good” at White House press secretary means you’re able to not get flustered on live television while spinning terrible news. Assessing them is merely judging a lying contest. Joe Biden’s last press secretary, Karine Jean-Pierre, got big-time flustered trying to pretend that Biden was not deep into senescence—low marks. Boo! Zacharia’s predecessor, Karoline Leavitt, did not get flustered pretending that Trump was doing a good and coherent job as president—flying colors. Zacharia, meanwhile, received the equivalent of a “see me after class” from the teacher in lieu of a grade. It’s just a parlor game. • The race in which we’re noticing a modest increase in Democratic alarm is Michigan’s Senate contest. Democrats did, after all, nominate a left-wing candidate in a purple state, and even in this environment, it wasn’t going to be easy getting him across the line. Where the worry has materialized is over his support with Black voters, among whom Abdul El-Sayed performed poorly in the primary. Our colleague Aymann Ismail does an excellent job explaining the nature of the concerns here. And both El-Sayed and Mike Rogers’ campaign decisions show that this softness in the El-Sayed coalition is blinking on their radars. El-Sayed has already campaigned with Kamala Harris in Detroit, and next Saturday, he’ll be campaigning with Barack Obama in Detroit. That’s heavy weaponry for turning out Black voters. And the Rogers campaign is paying more attention to Black voters in Detroit than Republicans typically do. It’s not that Rogers needs big inroads among Black voters. But he does need to maintain that softness for El-Sayed. • Back to the Texas Senate race: You know, money isn’t everything, although it’s definitely great. But is it serving its intended purpose of saving Ken Paxton? Well, a Truth Social post from Trump about how if Paxton loses, it will all be John Cornyn and Karl Rove’s fault doesn’t indicate that GOP internal polling has Paxton in a great position. (James Talarico isn’t broke, by the way. As it happens, he smashed the single-quarter Senate candidate fundraising record with a reported $64 million haul in the third quarter.) • How seriously should we take Georgia Sen. Jon Ossoff’s pledge from his debate this week? Asked whether he would commit to serving his full six-year term should he win reelection, he responded: “Yes, I will. I have zero interest in running for president. I’m not running for president.” This will come as a disappointment to the many fans that Sen. McDreamboat has picked up as he (probably!) cruises to reelection in a swing state. So can he get out of it? Sure. If he loses the presidential race in either the primary or the general, he will serve his full six-year Senate term, just like he said. And if he wins, then who cares whether those Georgia voters felt lied to—he’s gonna be president! But it’s also worth observing: He may be completely honest about how he has zero interest in running for president next year. Running for president sucks. • You know how last week we wrote about (a) how Senate control could all come down to Maine and how (b) debates don’t really matter, until the moment they do? It probably would have been best for Troy Jackson, who’s been criticized for hotheaded episodes involving women in the past, to make it through his fourth and final debate against Sen. Susan Collins without telling her to “shut up.” • Ossoff or No-ssoff, it’s still too early to tell who’ll have a leg up in the 2028 Democratic presidential primary, with dozens of candidates seemingly inter—ah, never mind. On Monday, the Justice Department raided a sitting congresswoman’s D.C. home to build a denaturalization case. Andrew Weissmann joins Dahlia Lithwick to explain the new depths being plumbed by Trump’s DOJ. ICE executed an early morning search of Rep. Ilhan Omar’s D.C. home—apparently targeting immigration statements made when she was 12 years old, by a father who is now deceased—all in service of a reported plan to strip her of her citizenship and deport her. Weissmann, lead prosecutor in Robert S. Mueller’s Special Counsel’s Office from 2017 to 2019 and chief of the Fraud Section in the Department of Justice from 2015 to 2019, walks Dahlia through what is shaping up to be another case of selective and vindictive prosecution, why it violates the department’s own 60-day election rule, and what it signals when post-Watergate guardrails established to prevent exactly these kinds of abuses have been dismantled. Weissmann and Lithwick also discuss the administration’s lawsuit against California over mail-in ballots—a case in which the evidence, like every prior non-citizen voting claim, collapses under scrutiny but serves its purpose before the courts can intervene. Plus: Trump’s ballroom grift, and why the only thing standing between another mid-term presidential pardon spree and total impunity for all the president’s men might be state criminal law. Trump Is So Desperate to Lower Gas Prices That He’s Throwing a Key U.S. Ally Under the Bus
2026-10-16

Trump Is So Desperate to Lower Gas Prices That He’s Throwing a Key U.S. Ally Under the Bus

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. It’s been a rough week for Ukraine, less because of anything happening on the battlefield and more because Donald Trump’s hot-and-cold relationship with the embattled country has once again gone frigid. Last Friday, Trump announced a plan to import millions of barrels of Russian diesel, which has been under U.S. sanctions since shortly after Russia started the war with Ukraine. Trump savaged his Ukrainian counterpart, Volodymyr Zelensky, over the weekend, saying, “I think it’s time for Ukraine to get a new president.” And this week, according to Reuters, the White House temporarily limited Ukraine’s access to a SpaceX-made satellite system it uses for battlefield communications. Those developments have understandably sent people who care about Ukraine’s future spiraling, convinced that the ostensible leader of the free world has shown his true cards. Buying Russian diesel would effectively subsidize Vladimir Putin’s war effort (even as U.S. taxpayers are already helping arm Ukraine). Ukraine’s satellite access seems to be coming back, but the interruption foiled attacks and cost Ukraine military drones. Zelensky appears to be losing patience, too, expressing betrayal after Trump demanded Ukraine stop attacking Russian energy infrastructure even as Russia continued to attack Ukraine’s. Seen in the context of Trump’s long-standing admiration for Putin, it’s hard to conclude that these developments are anything but bad for Ukraine. But is Trump’s turnabout permanent? At the risk of sounding like a contrarian, the truth is that he has waffled over the war more often than not. In some moments, like when he accused Zelensky of being insufficiently grateful for U.S. support last year, Trump has taken Putin’s side. But begrudgingly or not, Trump has also meaningfully helped Ukraine by agreeing to let it manufacture American weapon systems and by joining Europe in levying more sanctions against Russia over the summer. One reason Trump’s latest pivot against Ukraine might not last is that his diesel deal looks more like an act of desperation than a lasting strategic shift. As we’ve explained before, Trump is scrambling to lower U.S. gas prices, which his war of choice in Iran has elevated, ahead of the midterm elections. Despite his claim that prices “will be COMING DOWN, IN RECORD NUMBERS, AND FAST!,” the deal probably won’t do much to alleviate Americans’ pain at the pump before Election Day. But getting caught trying rarely hurts, and even small decreases could be enough to save endangered Republican candidates, who can use all the help they can get. Trump approaches international relations in personalist terms (he often describes foreign leaders as his friends, even when they aren’t), so his pivot back to bashing Zelensky is arguably a more worrying development. But a second reason to doubt whether it will last is that a surprising number of Republicans are pushing back. Several GOP members of Congress have joined Democrats in objecting to the diesel deal. Republicans facing tough races—including Ashley Hinson in Iowa and Mike Rogers in Michigan—have also criticized it. Republicans don’t stand up to Trump often, but their pushback over Ukraine has helped block some of his more damaging efforts, including trying to withhold U.S. weapons in his first term and threatening to pull the U.S. out of NATO in his second. None of that makes Trump’s turnabout, however long it lasts, any less of a betrayal. Whether his affinity for Putin owes to political desperation, autocratic envy, or profit motive (Trump’s negotiations to end the war reportedly include an oil deal that would enrich investors in his family’s ventures), it has already cost Ukrainian lives. But it may also come back to haunt Trump. By placing the U.S. in the position of supporting both countries’ competing war efforts, the president who campaigned on ending the fighting in 24 hours is arguably helping prolong it. Talk about winning the battle but losing the war. If you’re like now-former Trump press secretary Katie Zacharia and suddenly have some free time on your hands, consider my colleagues’ suggestions about how to spend your weekend: Understand the world with the help of the Muppets: In 2012, Dahlia Lithwick published a piece of cultural analysis that permanently altered how readers viewed their marriages, their workplaces, and the U.S. Supreme Court. At its core, her theory reveals that everyone is either a freewheeling Chaos Muppet (think Gonzo) or a steady, conscientious Order Muppet (think Kermit), and the world runs on the balance between the two. To celebrate Slate’s 30th anniversary, Dahlia revisited the theory with longtime Slate favorite June Thomas for a special Plus-only episode of Amicus. Take a trip to Blorple Falls: Back in Slate’s earliest years, the magazine took a detour from covering the real world and decamped to a quirky town in “West Carolina” known as Blorple Falls, populated by fictitious personae like conservative congressman Blanton Foghorn and his pop-star niece Mindy D’Stasio. Why would Slate go to the trouble of creating this alternate reality? Katy Waldman went down the rabbit hole to excavate this weird chapter of internet history. Rethink your birthday plans: As a kid, you get a birthday party. But when you grow up, you’re stuck with a birthday dinner. In 2008, John Swansburg argued that we should ditch this particular festivity: “Seems like a nice idea, the birthday dinner. It is not. It is a tedious, wretched affair.” Get sucked into this quicksand investigation: Quicksand used to dominate pop culture, but its heyday is firmly behind us. How and why did the tide turn against this sticky substance? In 2010, Daniel Engber took a deep dive into quicksand, approaching the topic from such diverse angles as its depictions in film and TV, the fetish scene devoted to it, and its shifting role in political rhetoric. It’s been another busy week of news, and I, for one, am ready to take a breather. But rest assured, we’ll be back in your inbox with more wild happenings on Monday. Enjoy the weekend! Texas Just Accidentally Set Up a Great Experiment About Whether Facts Matter at All in 2026
2026-10-16

Texas Just Accidentally Set Up a Great Experiment About Whether Facts Matter at All in 2026

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. It seems, finally, that the strange episode involving James Talarico and a nonsense story about a mental health facility has concluded. The story’s greatest promoter, the man who claimed to hold the smoking-gun evidence showing the Senate candidate had been institutionalized, declared on Thursday that the matter was over. “Frankly, this entire situation has been blown completely out of proportion,” wrote the social X user Rhein Amacher, somewhat bafflingly, given his own singular efforts to blow the situation up. “I’m not going to speculate beyond the information available or continue fueling a controversy based on an unverified claim. As far as I’m concerned, this matter is closed. It’s time to move on to more substantive issues facing voters.” It was an odd statement, as it didn’t quite recant the story, which has been thoroughly debunked. (“Everyone can listen and draw their own conclusions,” he said.) But other anti-Talarico accounts went quiet on the matter after that, and the social media campaign to portray Talarico as a man in a state of emergency, too mentally ill to represent Texas in the Senate, fizzled out. Afterward, we were left to grapple with what this bizarre saga had meant. This conspiracy theory had blown up fast, taken over the discussion of the Texas Senate race for a week at a crucially important time, and died abruptly. In the world of political conspiracy theories, it can often be mysterious why some take off and others flounder. This one did something else, reaching extreme heights but never achieving the sustained success of more entrenched conspiracy theories. It may have been the blatant partisan hunger of this one that took it on this trajectory, or some other dynamic in the community that sought to prop it up. But whatever the reason, the outcome of this strange episode may tell us something important about the limits of political tactics involving big and reckless lies. By virtue of being everywhere and then falling apart so completely, the hoax has become a natural experiment in whether a ridiculous conspiracy theory can move the needle in a close election—even after it’s thoroughly debunked. If you missed this whole saga, it began with the flu. On Sept. 30, after days of in-person campaign events, Talarico made a virtual appearance at an event, saying he was sick. He then apparently came down with a pretty bad case of the flu and made no public appearances until Oct. 9, when he appeared at the Austin City Limits music festival. In that time, a story took off on the right. It began with small accounts on social media speculating or claiming that Talarico was out not because he was physically ill but because he was mentally ill. On Oct. 7, his opponent Ken Paxton released an ad saying Talarico was “missing” and appeared at a rally with Donald Trump in San Antonio. At that rally, he started a chant of “Where is James Talarico?” Over the next three days, the story grew, amplified by a number of conservative media outlets; Republican figures talking of “sources” in the know, and a guest on Steve Bannon’s War Room podcast. On Saturday, the first piece of “evidence” emerged to feed the narrative: Caroline Wren, a GOP fundraiser, posted flight records showing that on Oct. 9, the day of Talarico’s reappearance in Austin, a private plane had flown from from Ohio to Austin—suspicious, apparently, because the Ohio airport wasn’t far from a mental health facility where a Houston judge had sought treatment in 2023. This plane theory had first come from an X account called “TickleTexts,” who did not seem to consider that the Austin City Limits festival draws visitors from all over the country. But the story really shot off the next day when the MAGA influencer Nick Sortor, seizing on Wren’s starting point, posted an audio clip that seemed to be of a somewhat confusing phone call with the Ohio mental health facility, the Lindner Center for Hope. The phone call had come from Amacher—the man now complaining the situation has been blown out of proportion—who said he had called the center, pretending to be a member of Talarico’s family. In the recording, Amacher pressed an employee after she said there was no record of Talarico; she eventually said “it look[ed] like” Talarico had been discharged that same day. But that made no sense: Talarico had been seen at Austin City Limits the day before that. Still, there was a brief high, before people had worked out the impossibility of this claim, in which conservative Texas accounts celebrated their triumph, with some calling on Talarico to exit the race. Elon Musk promoted the story to his 241 million followers. But that evening, the Lindner Center of Hope posted on X to correct the record: Talarico had never been a patient. Talarico later provided home security footage to the New York Times that showed he had been home the entire time. Most players involved were forced to acknowledge the whole story had been nonsense. It was strikingly bad luck for Talarico to fall ill for so long so close to the campaign’s conclusion. (According to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, Talarico’s diabetes can make the flu worse.) But once it was revealed that the whole narrative was built on lies and fantasies from minor X accounts, puffed out with baseless conjecture and easily disprovable claims, the glee seemed to turn to a rare incidence of embarrassment among right-wing media. Writing on Tuesday, Fox News, evidently smarting from the incident, tried to deflect blame back onto Talarico. His “viral hospital rumor has been debunked,” the account wrote in a post accompanying an article about the debunking. “But the bigger story may be why his campaign sat on the evidence while the conspiracy theory exploded across social media for days.” We are at an age of remarkable ubiquity, speed, and traction of campaign conspiracy theories, made all the worse by the tech companies’ retreat from content moderation. Republican leaders and conservative media and influencers constantly encounter rumors like this; those who amplified the Talarico-hospital story chose to allow the loudness of its believers to determine its legitimacy, and not the facts. The easiest read on this whole episode is that Paxton’s supporters are anxious and desperate to hold onto a previously safely red and symbolically important state. Talarico has been polling slightly above Paxton recently, and Republicans have poured enormous amounts of money into the campaign. It certainly looks desperate, going big on a conspiracy theory that anyone clear-eyed about the situation must have known would inevitably be debunked. Paxton has a whole host of scandals; Talarico only has J.D. Vance implying Talarico is gay and the time Talarico called God nonbinary. It makes sense that Paxton’s side would be antsy. For now, it’s difficult to determine how much the conspiracy theory will matter in November’s election. It’s possible that it was almost entirely self-contained in conservative conversations on X—people who were never going to vote for Talarico anyway. It’s also possible that it went beyond that, and that voters who lacked either the time or the inclination to follow the story through to its debunking will go to the polls next month believing they are choosing between a candidate who spent a week in a mental institution and one who didn’t. In what’s forecast to be a close race, even a marginal number of such voters could matter. (There’s a third possibility, perhaps one too outlandish to take seriously. And that’s that Paxton and his team may pay some sort of penalty for pushing a story that fell apart instantly upon making contact with a shred of reason. The lesson of Trump’s decade atop American politics is that the penalty for lying is generally nonexistent, but this may just be the exception.) Which of those three possibilities—the story flopping, working, or backfiring—emerges is an important barometer of where we stand in 2026, and whether the truth still has any purchase in our modern era. If a couple of weeks from now we’re hearing that voters were turned off by their fear of Talarico’s fragility or instability, we’ll know things are worse than ever. But if this whole episode is dismissed and laughed off as the misadventure of a few desperate attention-seekers, then it’ll be a sign: Even in one of the hottest battlegrounds in the country, reality still matters. Oh no, what happened to my OOHPod? Windows command prompt puts in a surprise appearance
2026-10-18

Oh no, what happened to my OOHPod? Windows command prompt puts in a surprise appearance

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-18

Visit Original Website
Pop quiz: somebody left a command shell logged in. What do you do? The Windows command line is a marvelous thing, beloved by administrators, but not quite so welcome when you're trying to get a parcel out of a locker. Today's example of Windows behaving badly comes from Ireland and one of the nation's OOHPod postal lockers. An eagle-eyed Register reader, trying to retrieve a parcel, found the locker screen instead teasing a command prompt. For the uninitiated, OOHPod is a service where people retrieve and deposit goods in lockers. Scan your ID (or enter it on a touch screen) and the appropriate locker door springs open and the item (or items) can be retrieved. This all works, assuming whatever is running behind the scenes isn't having a bit of a moment, as our reader experienced. Instead of a welcoming display on the locker's screen, our reader found a blank screen and what looked suspiciously like a minimized command-line window. "I tapped to maximize the window," they told us, "and a sudden command prompt appeared immediately afterward." A command prompt? More like an open invitation for mischief for the average Register reader. After all, while ordinary customers might have been flustered, this was surely an opportunity to teach the engineers who allowed it a lesson. Perhaps a cheeky FORMAT, or possibly the creation of a truly cursed batch file? Or something worse? This is surely what the comment section is made for. Unfortunately (or fortunately, depending on your perspective), no pop-up keyboard appeared for our reader to unleash havoc (not that they would dream of such a thing), and there was no handy socket to plug in a peripheral. The touchscreen accepted another finger jab, and the command window went away, replaced by the usual OOHPod interface, allowing packages to be collected as normal. It was the responsible thing to do, and the bank of lockers was left in a state where the next customer would not have to worry about what this odd cmd.exe thing was all about. However, the question remains. Would you have been so responsible? Or, if there was some way to enter commands, would you have tried to turn a minor bork into a life lesson for the engineer who let Windows give passersby a glimpse of its undercrackers? ® 'Hold my beer, have fun getting into your offices' Would you like personalized shopping recommendations based on that conversation you just had? www.theregister.com - Articles
Cybercrims go beyond stealing data to attack physical building systems
2026-10-17

Cybercrims go beyond stealing data to attack physical building systems

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
'Hold my beer, have fun getting into your offices' Late Sunday night, a still-unknown cybercriminal or group hacked thousands of remote-controlled refrigeration systems across hundreds of French grocery stores, spiking temperatures, spoiling food, and sparking fires. This is part of an ongoing trend of attacking physical systems that can do damage in the real world. In August, a ransomware attack disrupted building-management systems at a Canadian hospital, affecting door access controls, central HVAC monitoring, and the issuing of staff access cards. And three years ago, cyberattacks hit Las Vegas casinos, causing slot machines to go dark, preventing payment kiosks and ATMs from spitting out cash, and locking guests out of their rooms when the electronic keys stopped working. Not all digital intrusions remain in cyberspace, and attackers don’t need to encrypt files and steal data to cause chaos. Hitting physical systems often produces more severe consequences - and turns up the heat on executives to pay an extortion demand. “There was one gig where the customer was refusing to pay, and so the threat actor was like, ‘Hold my beer, have fun getting into your offices,’ and locked everybody out,” Tim Beasley, a senior incident response consultant at Semperis, told The Register. Beasley won’t name the customer, or provide any incriminating information, but said the incident response involved police smashing glass in addition to his team recovering compromised data. “The cops had to break in, and literally get into the building, breaking some windows, so the customer was able to finally get back in,” he said. We asked if the customer paid the ransom demand, and Beasley yelled: “Oh, hell, no!” A report published earlier this month by ServiceNow’s Armis Labs found increasing evidence of ransomware attackers using physical consequences as leverage in their intrusions. “Today’s BAS operate at the intersection of IT and OT worlds, creating unprecedented exposure,” the report said. Building automation systems - BAS - are the critical systems that control and monitor facilities’ HVAC, lighting, energy usage, and water consumption. They also oversee badge readers, door locks, surveillance cameras, fire alarm control panels, and other physical safety equipment. As such, BAS make very attractive targets for ransomware operators looking for high-pressure ways to extort organizations into paying multi-million ransom demands. These systems also appeal to government-backed goons and hacktivists looking to ensure maximum psychological and physical impact during destructive cyberattacks. Armis Labs found 73 percent of organizations own BAS that have been used in ransomware attacks, and this same percentage have known exploited vulnerabilities in at least one of their building automation systems. Additionally, 91 percent of BAS communicate over insecure protocols - opening up an even bigger attack surface for cyber-miscreants wanting to cause physical harm. “The primary thing that we see with any attack on building management systems or physical systems: It’s a great place for the threat actor to hide,” Michael Freeman, ServiceNow’s senior director of threat research, told The Register. “It's easier for them to compromise, and it's easier for them to hide in.” BAS and operational technology (OT) in general have fewer security controls than IT systems. Most require continual uptime, which makes them harder to patch or even scan for vulnerabilities and misconfigurations as this may lead to outages. “One case in particular, against a very large financial institution, the threat actors were staying low and quiet by attacking their BMS systems, and then pivoting into the IT environment,” Freeman said. The intruders were routinely caught - and booted from - the financial institution’s IT systems, so they built backdoors into the BMS to ensure persistent access to both OT and IT networks. “We're also seeing threat actors using AI to understand more building automation systems, especially ones that are using obscure OSs like VxWorks, older Unix-type systems,” Freeman said. As multiple security experts have told us in previous interviews, security through obscurity is dead. Not only does AI make it easier for attackers to find bugs – some very obscure and decades old - but it also means criminals no longer need to be OT experts to carry out destructive cyberattacks on critical networks and facilities. Instead, they can use AI to learn all about the obscure protocols and proprietary hardware and software that historically made industrial control systems black boxes. “It’s easier to understand what the controls are,” Freeman said, using the example of an attacker trying to take down a plane, or some similar catastrophic cyberattack. Historically, even advanced, government-backed hacking squads would “be quiet, because you don't know what changing certain settings is going to do,” Freeman explained. “Now you can use AI to say, ‘If I compromise a weight and balance system for airlines, all I need to do now is move this number here to this number, and I just told the system to load heavy stuff on the right side of the plane instead of equal distribution.'” Freeman told us he has seen attackers break into airlines’ OT gear in the past. “But they didn’t know what they were getting into, and so they left them alone,” he said. “Those do cause me concern because in the past I've helped to kick people out of those types of systems.” OT security provider Dragos tracks ransomware groups’ claims against industrial organizations on the criminals’ leak sites, along with publicly disclosed intrusions affecting manufacturing and other critical sectors. To be clear: claims on leak sites aren’t always accurate or even true, and the claim itself doesn’t mean operations or control systems were hit. Since 2022, however, “the numbers have been consistently increasing,” Lexie Mooney, senior threat analyst at Dragos, told The Register. The cybersecurity firm tracked 995 ransomware attacks against industrial orgs in 2023, a 49.5 percent increase from 2022. That number jumped more than 87 percent in 2024. Active ransomware groups targeting industrial orgs also grew in 2024 to 80 groups, up from 50 the year before. Dragos tracked 119 ransomware groups impacting more than 3,300 industrial organizations in 2025, and the incidents have steadily increased every quarter this year: 1,020 incidents in the first quarter of 2026 and 1,140 incidents in Q2. “The vast majority of cases are opportunistic,” Abdul Alamri, a senior threat analyst at Dragos, told us. “In some of the cases that we have seen an operational impact, either physical systems or an OT operation, it’s just the nature of the opportunism on the part of those ransomware groups.” This is partially due to the complexity of OT systems, he added. “Ransomware groups want to disrupt operations, and put pressure on the victims to pay the ransom,” Alamri said. “They don’t want to invest that time and effort to target complex systems." It’s usually quicker and easier for financially motivated crims to affect operations via IT systems - as the extortionists did in the August 2025 Jaguar Land Rover (JLR) breach, which halted manufacturing and crippled business operations for months. The other side of this complexity, however, is that many organizations themselves still lack visibility into their OT environments. Dragos estimates that fewer than 10 percent of OT networks worldwide currently have sufficient network monitoring in place. Plus, in 30 percent of incidents the security firm responded to in 2025, the investigations did not begin with a detection alert, but with someone noticing that “something seemed wrong” in operations. This lack of visibility, combined with threats from AI, worries Alamri. “If you compare the risk that we have seen from humans, to what we are about to face in the near future with AI agents who might continuously try to break into those systems - it’s a pretty dark future if we don't take the right measures to protect ourselves against it.” As organizations get better at patching and securing IT systems, attackers will likely look to internet-connected OT systems where digital compromises have real-world consequences, according to Matt Hayden, VP of cyber and emerging threats at General Dynamics Information Technology. “We all anticipate that as we get better at reducing the IT low-hanging fruit, OT will become a vector of much more scrutiny and much more attention,” Hayden told The Register. Before joining GDIT, Hayden served as the assistant secretary for cyber, infrastructure, risk and resilience policy at the US Department of Homeland Security. “There is a big focus on the hardware being connected for the purpose of compliance - and to make sure that, in the name of compliance, we aren't opening back doors, to devices that traditionally wouldn't have to be connected to facilitate their service,” he said, using refrigeration units as an example of one such system that is now internet-connected for compliance purposes. This was prior to the French supermarket hacks. Refrigeration units’ monitoring systems connect to the internet so their owners and operators can prove to regulators that the units maintain their uptime and temperature in accordance with regulations. “So if the health department comes by, they say, ‘Look, my refrigerator was at 27 degrees for the last 72 days,' and they have a check mark, and they get their A plus. But that simple connectivity is also a vector in which that particular refrigerant unit could be targeted for disruption,” Hayden said. About a week after our interview, criminals did exactly that. “The recently reported exploitations of commercial refrigeration units represent a significant example of devices without digital value, no sensitive data or extended enterprise access, being exploited by being connected to the internet with unpatched firmware,” Hayden told us in a subsequent email. He said he expects the attribution to be “less nation-state, and more likely those looking for targets of opportunity to identify what could be done with the unpatched systems.” “The consequences are food supply degradation at a small scale, but it is an important example of what could happen at scale very quickly with more consequential operating equipment,” Hayden said.® Pop quiz: somebody left a command shell logged in. What do you do? Amazon wants you to Bee-lieve wearable AI can recall it all for you
2026-10-17

Amazon wants you to Bee-lieve wearable AI can recall it all for you

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
Would you like personalized shopping recommendations based on that conversation you just had? Amazon wants to ensure you have a personal AI that you can carry with you anywhere, listening in on your conversations so it can remind you of key details – and give you personalized shopping recommendations, of course. The global online bazaar has unveiled two new AI-based products from Bee, a startup in wearable devices that it acquired last year in a deal said to be worth $300 million. Bee’s original product for Amazon was tried out and sort of given approval by friend of The Register Corey Quinn, Chief Cloud Economist at Duckbill, earlier this year. One new gizmo, Bee Frontier, is designed to be worn like a watch or one of those fitness trackers, although to our mind it looks more like you are wearing a doorbell. The other, Bee Eclipse, is a slimline and lightweight device about the size of a credit card that attaches to the back of your phone magnetically, like a limpet. Both are intended to listen in to your conversations and help you remember important details, like when you are meeting your friend for a pint later and at which hospitality outlet. The devices take note of the details you might otherwise forget, and then turn them into useful conversation summaries, to-dos and reminders, Amazon says. The pair of devices feature a single button to turn on or off, and a visible indicator that lights up when one is listening in and its AI is summarizing the conversation. When enabled, the eavesdropper gathers context from your conversations to provide insights and recommendations. Summaries of your conversations can be accessed via a Bee mobile app on iPhone or Android (linked via Bluetooth), or an all-new desktop app. These can be organized using templates for things such as lectures, or users can just review memories from the past few days to see what you discussed. The Bee devices pick up on trends or themes that recur, and recommend personalized goals and actions. That sounds alarm bells for us, as it suggests the AI is designed to get to know you, and what you like. Along with the new hardware, Bee has now gained the ability to connect to Amazon’s Alexa+ AI assistant and Amazon.com, which means that you can ask Alexa for a meeting summary - or get personalized shopping recommendations from Amazon based on what your personal earwig has gleaned. However, Amazon says that the latest Bee products respect user privacy, as audio is processed into insights in real time and not stored, while transcripts are encrypted and only the user can access them. Bee Frontier is claimed to have IP67 water resistance, up to seven days of battery life, and has a USB-C charging port. Bee Eclipse also has a USB-C port but is said to last for more than 21 days of use from a single battery charge. Both devices come in a choice of colors, with Bee Frontier available now for $74.99 and Bee Eclipse due to be available shortly for $99.99. ® Pop quiz: somebody left a command shell logged in. Get in the bin! UK Space Agency wants public to name its debris removal mission
2026-10-17

Get in the bin! UK Space Agency wants public to name its debris removal mission

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
ClearSpace UK aims to be operational in 2029 with public's suggestion etched on the side of the spacecraft The UK Space Agency needs a name for its Active Debris Removal Mission and has decided that the great British public are the people for the job. The agency confirmed the operation earlier this week and selected ClearSpace UK to lead it. The plan is to remove two UK-registered satellites from Low Earth Orbit, with the clean-up due to begin in 2029. The UK government is backing the effort with more than £60 million, and it expects the disposal to be completed by 2032. The spacecraft will grab each satellite, drop it into a lower orbit for disposal by burning up in the Earth's atmosphe, then climb to a higher one to await its next job. Less suicide dive, more cosmic bin round. And because it’s refuellable, the UK government says it "can help make a difference and sweep space for years to come." Launch is planned from SaxaVord Spaceport in Scotland. But first, the quest needs a name. The Active Debris Removal Mission hardly trips off the tongue, and the UK Space Agency gave inspirational examples of other project names, such as NASA's Mercury program (named after the Roman god) or Tim Peake's Principia mission (inspired by Sir Isaac Newton's Philosophiæ Naturalis Principia Mathematica). Oddly, the agency didn't mention the poll to brand a scientific research ship, which was eventually named Sir David Attenborough rather than Boaty McBoatface, the most popular suggestion. The tag must consist of two words – so no "Itsy bitsy teeny weeny orbit debris clear machiney" – and 150 words to explain the name and how it reflects the theme. So no backronyms, for example Broom (British Robotic Orbital Object Manipulator). Starry McBinface? Obviously, swear words are not allowed, nor is anything deemed "potentially offensive" or political. Using anything that might attract copyright or intellectual property lawyers is also out. Letting the public suggest a name feels potentially riskier than the mission itself, though it should generate some engagement. It's a high-risk, high-reward campaign; there are plenty of dead satellites cluttering orbit, and the ability to remove them is becoming increasingly important. Rob Atchison, Managing Director of ClearSpace UK, said, "Our next step is to design, build and test a spacecraft that can get close, safely, to an object never designed to be caught, and take control of it." If you're getting a sense of déjà vu about this expedition, you're probably right. The European Space Agency (ESA) announced ClearSpace-1 in 2019, which the agency called "the first space mission to remove an item of debris from orbit" with a launch set for 2025. At the time, ClearSpace founder and CEO Luc Piguet said: "This is the right time for such a mission." In latest announcement, Piguet called the UKSA's selection "an important milestone." This is far from the first mission to grapple an object in orbit. Most recently, Katalyst Space sent its LINK spacecraft to rescue NASA's Swift observatory. That was intended to capture the Swift spacecraft and raise its altitude, but failures with LINK doomed it. Perhaps things might have gone better if the public had been given an opportunity to name the spacecraft? ® Pop quiz: somebody left a command shell logged in. What do you do? Open-weight models win hearts, minds, and wallets
2026-10-16

Open-weight models win hearts, minds, and wallets

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
They handle 71.5% of Vercel gateway tokens and nearly a quarter of estimated spending Open-weight models more than quadrupled their share of spending through Vercel's AI Gateway between June and September, when they processed more than seven in 10 tokens. "In August, open-weight models processed the majority of AI Gateway token volume for the first time," the biz said. "The money is now following. Open weight's share of spend has more than quadrupled over the last three months, from one in twenty gateway dollars in June to nearly one in four in September." AI Gateway, like OpenRouter, acts as an intermediary that allows customers to purchase tokens from different AI providers through a common interface. While it works with popular harnesses like Claude Code and Codex and provides access to proprietary and open-weight models, it doesn't see usage through other gateways or from customers who access AI models directly from vendors or through on-premises hosting. While gateway spending in September was about 13x higher than recorded in December 2025, the average price per token was about 47 percent lower, according to Vercel. At the same time, almost one in three teams changed their most-used AI provider in September, suggesting that market competition may be limiting vendor lock-in. The most popular model in September was TypeSafe AI's Jev. In just 14 days, it reached 35 percent of paid teams using Vercel's AI Gateway, the company said. Its popularity didn't generate much spending due to the model's architecture – usage accounted for only 1 percent of the month’s tokens and 0.15 percent of spending. US-based models from Anthropic and OpenAI still attract the largest portion of spending in September, with 11.03 percent going to Claude Opus 5.5, followed by GPT-6 Astra at 4.86 percent. But models from Chinese AI providers have become more popular. DeepSeek V4.1 Flash ranked third in spending, at 4.19 percent. More than two-thirds of the September spending on Chinese models went to US providers serving those models, up from less than a tenth in December 2025, Vercel said. Moonshot AI, maker of Kimi, captured 3.6 percent of gateway tokens and 13.2 percent of spend in September; DeepSeek took 58 percent of tokens for just 6.6 percent of spend; and Z.ai, maker of GLM-5.3, took 7.2 percent of tokens and 4.5 percent of spend. So efforts to ban or limit open-weight models, something considered by the current US administration, would affect American businesses serving those models and their customers. Even if the US continues to dither on AI policy, trade barriers affecting AI models may alter market dynamics. China on Friday proposed adding 40 technology items to its export control list, 23 of them related to AI. The Associated Press notes that Moonshot’s Kimi K3 and Alibaba’s Qwen 3.8-Max are among the models that meet the parameter threshold set by Chinese authorities, though the official message did not identify affected models. "This introduces significant uncertainty for US businesses that host or rely on Chinese models," Chris McGuire, a senior fellow for China and emerging technologies at the Council on Foreign Relations, said in a social media post. "Particularly given that Chinese AI labs are moving toward licensing the use of open-source models to generate revenue, the Chinese government stating that it has the ability to curtail those licensing agreements at any time should make US businesses more hesitant to rely on those models going forward." McGuire said the US needs to adopt a comprehensive regulatory framework governing the use of US AI technology overseas and include Chinese AI models used or hosted in the United States in its domestic AI regulations.® Pop quiz: somebody left a command shell logged in. Benchmarked: Nvidia's RTX Spark notebook delivers serious local AI compute, at a price
2026-10-16

Benchmarked: Nvidia's RTX Spark notebook delivers serious local AI compute, at a price

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
El Reg digs its claws into HP's N1X-powered OmniBook Ultra 16 Nvidia's RTX Spark partner systems are here, and spec-for-spec they've got all the ingredients for a hit: a potent CPU, blisteringly fast integrated graphics, and up to 128 GB of unified memory in a lightweight, ultra-premium package that's ready to run demanding models locally. But with prices for these systems starting around $2,600 and going well north of $6,000 for the top spec trims, the one thing RTX Spark isn't is a good deal. However, for the right person, by which we mostly mean AI enthusiasts, researchers, and/or creative professionals who need tons of video memory and don't want to get lost in Apple's walled garden or would prefer Nvidia's CUDA ecosystem over AMD's ROCm, the RTX Spark still has a lot to offer. As such, we'll be largely focusing on the local agentic AI use cases Nvidia and Microsoft are pushing for these "builder-class" systems. The Register had the opportunity this week to put the OmniBook Ultra, HP's unique spin on the RTX Spark platform, through a series of AI benchmarks to see just how well it performs. And spoiler alert: you'll be hard pressed to find a faster Windows notebook for local AI. But before diving into performance, let's take a closer look at the hardware itself. Lifting the lid on the OmniBook Ultra 16, the first thing you'll notice is the gorgeous 2880x1800 pixel OLED touchscreen with a 16:10 aspect ratio. Touch screens on professional notebooks can be a contentious topic. You either love them or hate them, but at the end of the day, if the idea of fingerprints obscuring your work gives you pause, no one is forcing you to use it, and we're happy to see it here. The OmniBook Ultra's display is mounted to a surprisingly stiff hinge mechanism mounted to the top of the chassis. This has the benefit of keeping the hinge out of the way of the notebook's ample exhaust, which is kind of important because the 20-core N1X system-on-chip powering our review unit runs hot. The system's ample venting keeps most of that heat concentrated around the base of the display and for the most part, away from the keyboard. Speaking of keyboards, the OmniBook Ultra's digit fidgets are a bit of a throwback. Instead of the now-ubiquitous chiclet keys popularized by Apple in the early '10s, HP has gone for a more traditional notebook keyboard, which we found refreshingly easy to type on. Keyboards of course are subjective so if there's any question on whether this one is right for you, it might be worth a trip to your local big box store to give one a good pecking before you commit. Framing the keyboard are a pair of speaker grilles backed by four Cirrus Logic amplifiers which sound excellent for a Windows notebook, but can't quite match the clarity and bass of Apple's MacBook Pros. With that said, we don't expect anyone will be disappointed by the OmniBook Ultra's speakers. Directly below the keyboard is a haptic trackpad that is as precise as it is enormous. No product in this price class should be shipping with a diving board mechanism in 2026, so it's nice to see HP didn't cheap out on us. Moving to the sides of the notebook, you'll find a refreshingly comprehensive set of ports including: Our only real critique here is that the OmniBook Ultra, like other laptops, lacks a full size SD card slot. MicroSD cards are convenient, but they're not widely used in professional environments and we'd have preferred to see a full size SD card reader or better yet a CFexpress card reader. However, it's rare if not impossible to see these on notebooks. The chassis itself weighs in at 4.89 pounds and is milled from aluminum with a remarkably premium-feeling finish on the palm rest, lid, and bottom panels. HP has done a nice job building a notebook that feels as premium as it is powerful. While the OmniBook Ultra is an objectively nice machine, the main attraction of any of the RTX Spark systems is going to be the N1X chip powering them. The SoC features a MediaTek-derived CPU chiplet that's melded using advanced packaging and Nvidia's NVLink Fusion chip-to-chip interconnect to a Blackwell GPU. The N1X comes in two flavors: An entry level version with 18 CPU cores and a GPU with 5,120 CUDAs. Meanwhile, the highest-end SKU — the one found in our review unit — features up to 20 CPU cores and a GPU with 6,144 CUDA cores. If you're wondering, that's exactly the same number as found in Nvidia's RTX 5070 just with access to a whole lot more memory. We'll note that not all of those CPU cores are created equal. Like most Arm-based SoCs, the N1X contains a mix of big and little cores. Specifically, it has an equal complement of Arm X925 and Cortex A725 cores. While all of this sounds impressive, it's worth noting that the chip really isn't all that new. The N1X is based heavily on the GB10, which launched in the Linux-based DGX Spark over a year ago. With that said, there have been some improvements. At Hot Chips 2025, we learned the GB10 would support memory up to 9,400 MT/s. But, when the DGX Spark finally arrived that fall, it was only sporting 8,533 MT/s memory. The N1X rectifies this by boosting memory bandwidth to around 300 GB/s. Higher memory bandwidth usually translates to faster token generation in AI inference and agentic workloads. So, while a fairly modest boost in bandwidth over the DGX Spark's 273 GB/s, it's still a meaningful one. There aren't many modern workloads that really need 128 GB of unified memory, but local AI certainly is one of them. While you can certainly get away with less, it opens the door to models with a decent shot at replacing something like Claude Code or Codex. In fact, Microsoft believes that the future of Windows will be that of Copilot+ PCs that can run a mix of local and remote models with prompts routed between them based on things like complexity or privacy. Redmond is calling this technique hybrid intelligence. But even with 128 GB of shared memory on board, you're still somewhat limited on what models can actually fit. That's because, unlike on Linux, which allocates memory dynamically to the DGX Spark's GPU as needed, Windows isn't quite asur testi smart. The HP OmniBook Ultra arrived with a 50:50 split with 64 GB dedicated to the CPU and GPU respectively. This can be reconfigured as needed, but it's not exactly ideal. The good news is that, with the right software, you can get around this. Llama.cpp, one of the most popular local LLM inference frameworks, allows for overflowing into CPU memory as needed and, since the CPU and GPU share the same memory, the performance penalty isn't nearly as stark as it would when using a dedicated GPU. El Reg is excited to bring you more hardware coverage like this, but we're only just getting started. Ideally we'd pit the RTX Spark-based OmniBook Ultra against a similarly specced AMD Strix Halo notebook or Apple M5 Max MacBook Pro. Unfortunately we don't have those systems in our database just yet. In their place we're including performance figures for the DGX Spark and HP's Strix Halo-based Z2-Mini G1A. But because those aren't notebooks, we recognize it's not an ideal comparison. Having said that, you have to fit a lot more than just the model's weights into memory. For agentic AI harnesses like Claude Code, models need to support large context windows with 128,000 tokens being a sweet spot these days. Depending on the model, that can chew up an awful lot of memory. There is also the issue of the available models. While you could theoretically cram a 200 billion parameter model onto an RTX Spark using Llama.cpp, there aren't really many models that size. Since Nvidia's RTX Spark platform ships with Windows, we conducted all of our tests using the v0.6.0 build of Llama.cpp across five models developed by Meta, Google, and Alibaba in sizes ranging from around 26 to 122 billion parameters. Our first test simulates a chatbot-like environment tasking each system and model to process and generate a 256 token input and output. There are two metrics worth paying attention to here: Time to first token (TTFT) measures the time in milliseconds required for the GPU to process the prompt and generally reflects the GPU's compute performance. Tokens per second (Tok/s) is a little more obvious and reflects how quickly the system can churn out tokens once the prompt has been processed. Also called decode, token generation is usually dependent on memory bandwidth, so faster memory tends to perform better here. Across our test suite, we see performance scales neatly with model size and active parameter count. Pitted against AMD's Strix Halo and Nvidia's earlier DGX Spark systems, which, again, aren't ideal comparison points since they're mini PCs with more robust cooling systems, the HP OmniBook Ultra performs quite well. In Qwen3.6-35B-A3B — the fastest model we tested on any of the systems — the N1X's faster memory did give it an edge over the DGX Spark in token generation. The GB10 does appear to offer slightly higher compute performance, perhaps due to better thermals. Both Nvidia-based systems offer better performance than AMD's Strix Halo platform in our tests, though this shouldn't come as a surprise. On paper, Strix Halo offers less performance and memory bandwidth than either Nvidia chip and we've repeatedly shown this in testing over the past year. AMD's saving grace is that it has consistently offered better value. While our single batch and context scaling benchmarks are useful for quantifying system performance across models, they aren't necessarily representative of how you're likely to use local models today. If our numbers seem a little low compared to other benchmarks for the N1X and GB10, that's because our single batch results don't take advantage of prefill caching or speculative decoding. If you're not familiar, prefill caching helps to speed up prompt processing time for information the model has already seen before. Speculative decoding meanwhile involves using a small draft model to predict the outputs of a larger one. In highly repetitive workloads, like agentic coding, speculative decoding speeds up token generation by 2-3x without any loss in quality. AI agents, like code assistants, pass over the same prompts, code bases, and resources recursively, which makes them prime candidates for both techniques. In real world benchmarks, the overall line up remains unchanged to see high overall performance from these systems. This test measures system performance when running an agentic coding workflow using Alibaba's Qwen Code (not to be confused with the Qwen-Coder family of models) harness and measures how quickly each system and model can solve a bin packing problem. This addresses one of the more interesting phenomena that has cropped up around agentic AI. Faster token generation doesn't necessarily translate to a faster time to solve. Smarter but slower models can still be faster if they use fewer tokens to arrive at the correct answer. And we see that here. Large models like Qwen3.5-122B-A10B ran slower on our test systems, generating tokens at roughly half the rate of the smaller 35B parameter model. Yet, the median time to solve was roughly 40 seconds faster on the larger model because it needed only a fraction of the tokens. Having said that, given the extreme cost of memory right now, we believe the sweet spot for local AI code assistants is somewhere in the 20-60 billion parameter range, which is small enough to run on less expensive 64 GB versions of Nvidia's RTX Spark systems. As you might expect, any new platform is going to have a few growing pains at launch. It was certainly true when Intel introduced its performance and efficiency cores and when Qualcomm brought its Arm-based Snapdragon X Elite SoCs to the PC. Nvidia's RTX Spark platform is no different. During our testing, we observed issues with thermal throttling or rather an apparent lack thereof. Under a constant AI inference load, we experienced occasional lock ups and, on one occasion, graphical artifacts as the GPU exceeded 90 degrees Celsius. AI inference and training are some of the most resource-intensive workloads ever developed and to call them resource viruses wouldn't be inaccurate. So it's not surprising that a sustained inference load over the course of several hours might overwhelm the thin-and-light's thermal management system. Under real world conditions we don't expect this will be an issue. Even a few seconds between prompts was enough for the notebook to remain stable and artifact free. With that said, if you were hoping to fine tune LLMs, you might want to look at Nvidia's DGX Spark or any one of the other GB10-based AI workstations from HP, Dell, Lenovo, and others. Their larger chassis and thermal management systems make them far better candidates for the often hours-long process of fine tuning models. Software support for PyTorch also happens to be a bit better on Linux than on Windows. Beyond a cooling system that's perhaps a bit optimistic for what is supposed to be a 140 watt SoC, the only glitch we ran into was the Cirrus Logic audio outputs disabling themselves out of the blue. Thankfully all it took to get them running again was to uninstall them in device manager and reboot. The OmniBook Ultra 16 and the rest of the RTX Spark lineup occupy a rather niche segment in 2026. With memory prices at an all time high, the system spans the gamut from ultra-premium thin-and-light in its base config and mobile workstation at the upper end of the spectrum. The OmniBook Ultra 16 is an objectively strong performer and an extremely well built one at that. But at around $3,200 for the base model OmniBook Ultra 16 with the N1X and $6,229 as tested here, none of these systems are cheap. Had Nvidia and its partners launched these notebooks a year ago at between $2,000 and $4,000, the story might have been different. Unless you've drunk the AI Kool-Aid and are buying an RTX Spark system for local AI development or happen to have a particularly VRAM hungry workload that makes you money, these systems are going to be a tough sell for the same reason it's hard to recommend workstation components to the average consumer. Most folks can't take full advantage of them and their money is usually better spent elsewhere. And if you can and you're willing to give up a little performance, AMD's Strix and Gorgon Halo-based notebooks and mini PCs are definitely worth cross shopping. As memory prices normalize, we do expect Nvidia's next-generation of RTX Spark systems to be both faster and more attractively priced. Unfortunately, we will wait a while for that to happen. The memory fabs expect DRAM supplies to remain constrained through at least 2028. We've got a whole lot more testing planned for the RTX Spark and HP's OmniBook Ultra, including a comprehensive set of CPU and gaming benchmarks, along with a deeper dive into agentic AI performance as we look to answer the question whether these systems can actually save you money. So far that argument isn't looking good. ® Pop quiz: somebody left a command shell logged in. What do you do? TikTok bans Musk documentary account
2026-10-17

TikTok bans Musk documentary account

The Verge | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
Larry Ellison’s TikTok has shut down the promotional account for Alex Gibney’s Elon Musk documentary. Larry Ellison’s TikTok has shut down the promotional account for Alex Gibney’s Elon Musk documentary. Just ahead of the wider release of Alex Gibney’s Musk documentary, TikTok has banned the film’s social media account from its platform. TikTok did not immediately respond to a request for comment. According to The Hollywood Reporter, the film’s distributor, Bleecker Street, was informed Friday afternoon that a “permanent suspension” was imminent. Bleecker Street has not yet responded to a request for comment from The Verge. While TikTok has no official affiliation with Elon Musk, part owner Larry Ellison appears in the film via a text message offering to help Musk fund his Twitter purchase. Last month, YouTube, Meta, X, and TikTok all faced backlash for rejecting ads for the film. YouTube and Google quickly reversed course, calling it an “error.” However, TikTok and X have refused to change their stance, and neither platform has allowed paid advertisements for the film. Banning the documentary’s account, however, marks a serious escalation, and one laced with a dash of irony — shortly before the account was removed, it posted that people should see the movie “before it gets shut down.” A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Mercedes-AMG’s first ground-up SUV is a 1,150hp electric beast
2026-10-17

Mercedes-AMG’s first ground-up SUV is a 1,150hp electric beast

The Verge | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
It may even out-muscle the Porsche Cayenne Turbo Electric and Lucid Gravity. It may even out-muscle the Porsche Cayenne Turbo Electric and Lucid Gravity. Earlier this year, Mercedes’ AMG performance brand grabbed our attention with the GT four-door coupe, a souped-up sports sedan with three axial flux motors delivering over 1,150 horsepower. Well, now meet its beefier cousin, the 2028 Mercedes-AMG GT SUV, an equally powerful, ultra-fast-charging electric people-hauler. The AMG GT SUV (say that five times fast) marks the first time in the performance brand’s 59-year history that it has developed a full-size SUV entirely in-house from the ground up. Scheduled to hit the US next summer, the new EV is built on Merc’s high-performance AMG.EA architecture to combine sports-car handling with full-size SUV suburban practicality. It will be available in three trims — GT 53, GT 55, and the flagship GT 63 — with the top model delivering 1,153hp via the same three axial flux motors powering the four-door coupe. That beats the Porsche Cayenne Turbo Electric with Launch Control, which is sure to make for some awkward run-ins around Stuttgart. Thanks to an 800-volt architecture, the SUV supports blistering-fast charging speeds up to 710kW on the 128kWh battery (GT 63 / GT 55) and 600kW on the 106kWh battery (GT 53). Drivers can charge 10–80 percent in 12 minutes or gain up to 450km (around 280 miles) of WLTP range in just 10 minutes. The 128kWh battery trims will have an estimated max range of 435 miles. That battery, by the way? It utilizes F1-inspired technology with specialized NCMA cathodes, silicon-containing anodes, and cylindrical cells. An electrically nonconductive oil flows directly around each individual cell for uniform thermal management, enabling continuous high-power delivery and long battery life. Mercedes has quietly been on the vanguard of battery technology for a while now, with the AMG GT SUV just the latest example. The 710kW charging speed, in particular, could give Mercedes another edge over Porsche’s 320kW speeds. For a full-size SUV, the AMG GT sounds like it could handle like a vehicle half its size. Mercedes-AMG replaced the traditional mechanical roll bars with a newly developed suspension that uses active hydraulic elements to eliminate pitch and roll during acceleration, heavy braking, and cornering. Combined with three-stage air springs and standard six-degree active rear-axle steering, it purports to balance track precision with long-distance comfort. But just because it’s a beefy lad doesn’t mean it’ll tip the scales. The AMG GT 63 SUV weighs in at 5,941lbs, making it slightly lighter than the Lotus Eletre R (6,007lbs) and Lucid Gravity (6,100lbs), and nearly identical to the Porsche Cayenne Turbo Electric (5,960lbs). Silence is golden — except to a performance brand’s EV engineering team. That means piping in recorded sounds of real AMGs — in this case, the legendary AMG V8 and six-cylinder engines — to preserve the “emotional” AMG identity. Mercedes is proud that it’s using real engine sounds over synthetic EV tones, though some may disagree. The system even simulates mechanical gear shifts with paddle controls, offers adjustable sound profiles, and integrates physical seat vibrations — so you can still feel like there’s a real engine under that hood instead of a 78-liter frunk. The so-called AMGFORCE Sport+ drive program is offered as standard across all three trims of the Mercedes-AMG GT SUV. Inside, you’ll find the MB.OS platform on your 14-inch display, with an army of AI agents from ChatGPT, Microsoft, and Google at your disposal to boss around. Use them to set navigation, discover random locations, do web searches, tell bad jokes, or whatever else people use AI for in their cars. Merc’s own onboard AI system can handle the physical stuff, like adjusting HVAC, changing drive modes, or fiddling with the lighting — all through voice commands. Beyond voice and navigation, the AMG GT SUV applies AI to high-performance track driving through something called the Predictive Performance Manager. Mercedes says the PPM uses track-mapping data to dynamically throttle or boost electric motor output depending on topography, conserving battery energy on downhill or technical winding sections so full power is available on steep inclines and long straights. That could also mean faster, more consistent overall lap times by eliminating battery overheating and power throttling during track driving — something an AMG lover would likely appreciate. Mercedes didn’t release the official pricing yet, though we can expect something closer to its official launch. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. In 2024, Elon Musk promised to give away $1 million a day to a randomly selected registered voter in a swing state. That prompted legal challenges, raised questions about its legality, and drew lawsuits from two Arizona residents claiming the lottery was a fraud. Musk has now agreed to settle the two lawsuits filed in Austin, Texas, though the details of the settlement have not been made public. Philadelphia’s District Attorney sued to stop the giveaway, claiming that Musk was running an illegal lottery. During a hearing, Chris Gober, a representative for Musk’s America PAC, told the judge that “The $1 million recipients are not chosen by chance … We know exactly who will be announced as the $1 million recipient today and tomorrow.” That directly contradicted Musk’s claim that the drawings were random. The plaintiffs say they never would have signed the petition required to enter the giveaway if they had known it wasn’t random. Meta removes language that may have allowed it to scrape news sites
2026-10-17

Meta removes language that may have allowed it to scrape news sites

The Verge | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
Language that experts say allowed the scraping of news sites to train its AI has been removed from Meta’s terms. Language that experts say allowed the scraping of news sites to train its AI has been removed from Meta’s terms. After backlash, Meta is updating its terms again to remove language that suggested it could scrape content from sites, including news sites, to train its AI. Specifically, the following language no longer appears on the commercial terms page: … Meta may use any content from such domains, web pages, or URLs to provide, develop, improve, research, train, and maintain the integrity of, the Meta Company Products, services, and features provided under these Commercial Terms and any other applicable supplemental terms. Many, including Neiman Lab and James Grimmelmann, a professor of digital and information law at Cornell, interpreted it as an attempt to give Meta the right to use content scraped from sites to train its AI products for free and supersede blocking instructions in a site’s robots.txt file. Andy Stone, Meta’s vice president of communications, and spokesperson Daniel Roberts pushed back, saying that “There is no change to how Meta uses web crawlers; any suggestion to the contrary is false.” Today’s update to its terms and a new statement go further, removing the controversial language entirely. Roberts said in a follow-up statement to The Verge this morning: “We’ve heard you and want to clarify: the crawling terms we consolidated only applied to our ads and business products, and we’ve clarified that in the terms themselves. It does not change how Meta uses web crawlers. As always, content owners can use robots.txt to signal their preferences. This is not about scraping news sites, and these updates don’t give us new rights that supersede those preferences.” A free daily digest of the news that matters most.
